PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

A Youtzu man at Niagara, having been crossed In love, walked out to the precipice, gave one lingering look at Ike gulf beneath him, and-'-went home. THis body was found next morning—in bed. . WIFE (who is always ailing) : "You will bury me by the side of my first husband, won't you, John ?" Husband: "With pleasure, my dear.' SruArrs "Miss Elder is much older than I thought." Hunker! "Impossible !" Spratts : "Well, I asked her if she had read zEsop's Fables, and she said she read them when they first came out." FATHER "What would you advise me to do with my son? His pronunciation is perfectly terrible. Teacher : • Get him a position as porter at a railway station at once." A rovrsuiaL journal inserts the following in- genious puff: "We have no Pity for the man who blunders right into the arms of a fierce creditor simply because he is too mean to buy a pair of Highsite's glasses." DAUGHTER; "No, father, I cannot marry that man. He has red hair." Father: "But, my dear daughter' that objection ,joesn't amount to any- thing. Don't you notice that he is almost.. bald, and in a short time will not have a single red hair on his head ?"

CITIZEN : "Why are you trying to shoot that dog?" Policeman: "He's mad." "How do you know he's mad ?" "He refused wattr." "Lon- don water ?'' "Yes." "Bah ! That's no sign." Doctrou : "Well, my fine little fellow, you have got quite well again ! I was sure that the pills I left for you would cure you. How did you take them, in water or in cake?" "Oh, I wied them in - my pop-gun." Gnus, be very thy about marrying a man who -can suppress a fult-grown sneeze in church, it indicates a bull headed amount of will power which will interfere with your happiness some day. "I mi. you, Susan, that I will commit suicide if you don't have me." "Well, Thomas, as soon as you have given me that proof of your affection I will believe that you love me." "WHY, Tom, my dear toy, how much older you look !" "All right ! The fact is, I never was so old in all my life." HOSTESS: "Miss Telfer has no partner for this waltz; you will not mind dancing with her instead of with me?" He (anxious to be very obliging): "On the contrary, I shall be delighted." COUNT DE COSIMOPOLIS : "Zen mademoiselle do not feel ze indifference for me?" Miss Wealthful : " No ; I've been awfully interested in you since papa told me you were an adventurer. It's so jolly Tell me about your escapes and all that.* AT A SCHOOL EXAM. —lnspector : "What do you see above your head when you are in the open air ?" Scholar: "The sky." Inspector : "And what do you see when the sky is covered with clouds !" Scholar : "My umbrella." "THAT'S a very lovely baby of yours, Lawson. I wonder what he will develop into?" " Well, if we can judge of the future by the present, I think he will be a town crier when he grows up." "RICHES take unto themselves wings, and fly away," said the teacher, "'What kind of riches is meant ?" And the smart boy at the foot of the - class said "They must be ostriches." SUITOR : "Sir, you are undoubtedly aware of the object of my visit ?" Father: "I believe you desire to make my daughter happy. Do you really mean it?" Suitor: "Unquestionably." Father: "Well, don't marry her, then." "NOT To - DAY, THANK You!" Servant: "Please, ma'am, there's a poor man, at the door with wooden legs." Young Housewife: "Why, Babette, what can we do with wooden legs? Tell him we don't want any." MAGISTRATE (sternly) : "This is your sixth . appearance here." Impudent Prisoner:' "Quite right, guv'nor, go up top !" MRS. NICKER (whispering to her husband) : - "Oh, John, I believe there's a burglar in the room routing about your clothes !" Mr. N. : "Well, it doesn't matter. You've been through my pockets . already, haven't you." His: "And did you see Monte Carlo while you were at Nice ?' She: "No; papa called on him, I believe, but from his disappointed . appearance when he returned to the hotel, I ithink Mr. Carlo must have been out." MRS. STRONGMIND : "Why don't you go to -work?" Tramp: "Please, mum, I made a solemn vow twenty ySars ago, that I'd never do another stroke of work till women was paid th' same wages as men." Gets a trifle. Miss HAYDON : "Miss Palmer, I must ask you to explain yourself. I understand that you told Mrs. Palmer that my brother George was the black sheep of the family." Miss Palmer: "My dear Miss Haydon, it was merely a slip of the tongue, I assure you. I meant to say white sheep." "CAPITAL" CHAT.

Mrs. Norman, the charming wife of the new as- sistant editor of the " Chronicle." is the " The Girl in the Karpathians," the Menie Muriel Dowie, who not so very long ago, when she made her first ap- pearance on any stage. or rather platform, took the hearts of all the journalists by storm. The eulogies passed upon her face and figure, and—in- cidentially—upon the paper the Pressmen were sent to report, in the next morning's journals have pro- bably never been surpassed.

I may take this opportunity of mentioning that much misapprehension seems to exist as to the object of the understanding between this country and Russia—the other Powers, except the United States, being assenting parties—for intervention in certain contingencies in the war and in the peace to be concluded at its termination. The misappre- hension to which I refer is the basis of an article in the "Spectator," and is that the intervention is to be in the interests of China and against Japan. The latter, it is said, is not to be allowed to reap the full fruits of her victory, because the Powers wish to save China from the consequences of defeat. But this is a complete misunderstanding of the objects of the arrangement, which has its foundation in the unquestionable fact that the condition and future of China are subjects of international inte If Japan is to be allowed to begin the dismem- berment of China, the floodgates of ambition and greed for territory will be opened, and no one can tell where the process will end. If Japan gets Formosa or part of ;Manchuria, or both, Russia will want to alter her position to meet the altered cir- cumstances ; so will Great Britain, France, and perhaps other Powers, and the " scramble for China" may be the great calamity of the next century. None of the Great powers want this, not because it would aggrandise Japan, but because it might lead to years of war. We are not going to see the dismemberment of China just yet. The Powers are determined that Japan shall not be allowed,as part of the price of peace, to begin the process now or to hasten the evil day which may possibly be in the future. China will have4to pay for her defeat, 'but Japan will not be allowed to exact a price which would be paid by the rest of the world. As I have stated for months past, the peace will be an international, not a Japanese peace, for the international interests concerned are infinitely larger than those which are merely Japanese.

west.

The retiring Speaker, it is understood, will not take a peerage, thus breaking through a custom of over a century's duration. The fact is, Mr. Peel's private means are not large,being about two thous- and a year, for the late Sir Robert Peel left nearly everything to his eldest son, the present baronet. As Mr. Peel has a family, be does not think himself , rich enough to accept a peerage. He may possibly accept the Grand Cross the Bath.

A gentleman who, after two years' absence in America, recently returned to Germany, gives a sad account of the change which has taken place in the appearance of Prince Bismrack. " His eye," he says,"has now lost every trace of fire and sparkle,h as become dim, bleary, and vacillating. His herculean frame has shrunk and shrivelled to a pitiful degree. His step is tottering and feeble. "He looks what he is—a man at tbe very end of his life, with every cord, every muscle, every nerve worn te the break- ing point, and it will be very surprising indeed if his physician permits him to take any part in the monster festivities, extending over an entire week, which have been organised to commemorate his 80th birthday."

There is now no doubt that Mr. Gully will be the Ministerial nominee for the Speakership. The honourable member for Carlitle was practically in- formed some days since that, in the event of Mr. Courtney not wishing to be put forward, he would receive the support of the Government. The choice is regarded as an excellent one, not only in the 'louse of Commons but outside, and especially in 'legal circles, where Mr. Gully is highly esteemed. Those who know him well are persuaded that he will make an admirable Speaker,as he equally com- bines the "suaviter in inodo " with the " fortiter in re" elements,which cannot fail to make an ideal president of the representative Chamber.

The Conservatives are daily becoming more closely banded into a solid body against the claim of the Liberal Unionist to the seat at Leamington. Mr. Balfour has already discovered that his .good, valued, and well-intentioned letter in favour of the Sueaker's son was written without a due know- ledge, or at least a due consideration of the facts, and it is more likely want of thought than want of knowledge that dictated it; and the determination of the Conservatives to run Sir Matthew White Rid- ley for the Speakership, to the complete extinction of Mr. Courtney's chances,makes,in Stock Exchange parlance, a deal with the Liberal Unionists im- possible.

It comes to this that the Tories want both the Speakership and the Leamington seat; and it will he an edifying spectacle,as to the depth and intrin- sic value of the Unionist alliance, 'if a Tory and a Liberal Unionist candidate are pitted against each other. It will furnish a fertile theme for some of Sir William Ilarcourt's most effective jokes. As I have always pointed out, the alliance would not be worth three months' purchase were it not cemented by the Anti-Rome Rule policy of the two political sections ; and the two controversies which are now raging between them will•leave seeds of bitterness behind which must bear fruit in time to come.

-- Mr. Francis Stevenson and the Anglo-Armenian Committee are completing the arrangements for the deputation to the Foreign Office. '1 he deputation will include a large number of members of Parlia- ment of all parties, and also influential men not in Parliament. Many both inside and outside Parlia- ment have been extremely anxious to speak out on 1 this question, and are convinced that nothing will I wake up the country so uPich as a vigorous and unqualified debate in the House of Commons. The return of Mr. Gladstone has been looked to with much hope by those interested in Armenia. It is now, as it has been for a year past, unlikely that Mr. Gladstone will again speak in the House of Commons. But it is confidently expected that when a favourable opportunity offers he will not be unwilling to make one more great speech on behalf of mercy and purity and humanity in Ow aat. If he were now to strike the blow, as only he can, which would set the poor Armenians free from this revolting and detestable tyranny, it, would nobly I crown the long list of Mr. Gladstone's services to the 1 suffering and oppressed.

--- The Liberal Unionists who attended Monday's meeting with regard to the speakership are very reticent as to the preceedings, but certain fasts have leaked out which throw much light on the neneral situation. It is certain that when the meeting began there was n'thing like unanimity as to the proposal to throw Mr. Courtney over, but Mr. Chamberlain carried his point by a speech which left do doubt in the minds of the members present that they were in face of a serious crisis. Unless I am misinformed, he went so far as to say that the alliance of the two sections of the , Unionists party was in imminent danger, and that it could only be saved by the concession which he proposed.

It is beyond all doubt that be usod language of the most vigorous order as to the possible results of a split over the Speaksrship. As the Liberal Unionist leader and the Conservative leaders have been in . frequent communication in the last few days, Mr. Chamberlain wilt have spoken with full knowledge of the facts. It is now evident that 1 when the Liberal Unionist leaders went the length of issuing a communication to the press last week by implication supporting Mr. Courtney's candida- ture they. presumed a little too far, and were glad tomake a hasty retractation. But the tension con- tinues, and it may yet have important results. Even if the arrangement between the leaders on the sub- ject ot the Leamington election is allowed to hold good it will not have the approval either of the lccal Conservatives or of the Conservative rank and file in the House of Commons, who make no secret of the fact that they are "sick of being dictated to" by Mr. Chamberlain. There is every indication, in fact, that the bulk of the Conservatives would rather welcome a rupture. .

LADIES' LETTER.

President Cleveland never wears gloves. Russian lovers send a daily present to their fiancees. Every day two hundred thousand cigars are smoked in London. Until the close of the seventeenth century it was al- most impossible to obtain female dancers as ladies would not dance in public., so young men and boys were habited as women and took the female parts. Max O'Rell says that the Australians eat seven times a day ; tea and bread and butter at ' seven breakhst at half-past eight, lunch at eleven, dinner at half-past one, tea at three, supper at six, and a nightcap at'ten. Tea is the standard drink, and there are few hours in the day when the teapot is not brought into requisition. Ouida is almost penniless. Despite the stupendous sums of money she has drawn by her books during the past 50 years, she has got through it all and now her house and all her property in Italy have been sold to pay her debts. Everybody in Florence knows her by sight. She is small, with a seamed and wrinkled face, overhung with grey ringlets, and is afflicted with a distinctly bad temper. Her grandfather was a Frenchman, but her mother was English, and she was born at Bury St. Edmunds, in Suffolk. She took her nom de guerrs of Ouida, from the childish pronounciation of her real name Louisa, by her little niece.

The Empress Charlotte of Mexico,sister of the King of the Belgians, and first cousin of Queen Victoria' is said to be dangerously ill of pneumonia. Her death would, perhaps, be a happy release, as she has been hopelessly insane ever since her husband, who (were he alive) would now be heir-presumptive of the Austrian Crowns, was , shot in Mexico in 1867.

How Women Should Dress Their Hair.

The " Windsor Magazine" for March gives a number of useful hints from a well-known West-end hairdresser has to how women should dress their hair. Amongst other advice of interest and importance to women who study the latest and most becoming modes, he explains that "to wear the hair brushed right off the face demands a perfect forehead. If the forehead be very broad, the hair should be drawn tightly at the sides. If the back of the head be unduly developed, the hair should be slightly raised behind and dressed flatly. If the cheek bones are too prominent, fluff out the hair. For a large face, dress the hair high and broad, never narrow on top, and a girl with a face of this description and full,fat cheers should never wear a pointed fringe, which throws out and accentuates all her defects, increasing the heavi- neis of her natural appearance to the most unbecoming extent. For a very wide, well shaped forehead the hair should be softly turned back in the centre and fringe on each temple. If the forehead be less good, the centre piece may be waved and slightly puffed forward so as to improve its shape. A very hard and masculine face looks best perhaps with the hair cut short and curled. A girl with a pretty neck should, at least for the evening,dress her hair low en catogesn. A woman whose neck is short should give it length bv having her locks combed or twisted straight up at the back and arranged as high on her head as possible."

Eczema.

Eczema, or '• Tatter," as it is sometimes called, is the commonest of all skin diseases. It is, says " The Family Physician," an inflammation of the skin, at- tended with a watery discharge,which on drying,,stiffens the linen with which it comes in contact. It is always moist atsome period of its course, and may be regarded as a catarrh of the skin—a kind of bronchitis of the skin, in fact. It is generally constitutional, but may arise from purely local causes, and may even depend on the presence of some parasite, such as itch. It not in- frequently complicates or is associated with other skin diseases. It occurs on all parts of the body, but its com- monest seats are behind the ears, at the corners of the mouth, and on the head. It is cemmon at all ages, in- fants and young children being especially liable to it. It is said to be most prevalent in the spring and autumn ; bkut it is a chronic affection, and one meets with it all the year round. It may be brought on by anything that irritates the skin, especially when the constitution is debilitated. It generally begins in the form of slightly reddened patches, on which speedily appear minute watery heads or vesicles, which burst, leaving a - all thin crust of a bright yellow colour. In bad cases the patches are not only vividly red, but are swollen, and feel hot and tense. Sometimes the discharge is thick, and cakes almost as soon as it is poured out,form- ing nasty, unsightly scabs. After a time the patches may cease discharging, and this makes the complaint rather less easy of recognition; but on inquiry, it will be found that they used "to weep." When ecz-ma at- tacks the heod the viscid secretion mats the hairs togeth- aer'and keeps them wet, sticky,and entangled. All forms of eczema are apt to become chronic,and it is often an in- tractable and distressing affection. It is prone to recur after complete recovery, and at each return it becomes less amenable to treatment.

" WITCHCRAFT " IN IRELAND.

ALLEGED HORRIBLE MURDER. A revolting crime is reported from Clonmel. A married woman named Cleary having mysteriously disappeared, a man named Simpson told the police that while in the house in which Mrs. Cleary, her husband, and her father, Patrick Boland, lived, he saw her subjected to frightful ill-treatment. A number of other men were in the house at the time, and from what he heard he cameto the conclusion that they thought Mrs.Cleary was a witch. In order to exercise the supposed witch, they held her over the fire until she was literally burnt to death. Her body was then removed from the house, and a police search party found it buried in ' a ditch some distance away. Her husband, her father, and nine other persons are now in custody charged with causing her death. At a special court held in Clonmel on Tuesday some remarkable evidence was given. The prisoners ihclude the husband and father of the deceased and a local herbalist. The evidence of a woman named Burke was to the effect that she was nursing the deceased, who suffered from nervous excitement and bronchitis. Her husband believed she was a witch and •ob-ained some herbs from a herbalist. While, the prisoners held the sick woman her husband forced her to take the herbs, and caused her to be held over a fire until, in the name of God, she declared that she was not his wife. The following night the proceedings were repeated, but the deceased refused to conform to her husband's requests, whereupon he knocked her down, stripped her, poured paraffin over her, and then set fire to her body with a lighted stick from the tire. She burned to death in the presence of six male and two female relatives, who did nothing to save her. They, however, remonstrated with the husband, who declared that he was not burning his wife but a witch, and that she would disappear up the chimney. He collected the charred remains in a sheet, and buried them in a dyke near his house, where they were found a week later by the police. The prisoners, who were remanded, were hissed and groaned at by an excited crowd.

Virc PARTED AT THE ALTAR;

Ly LAURA JEAN LIBBEY,

author of "A Mad Betrothal," "lone," "Parted by Fate,' " ploribet 8 Lover," etc., etc.

CHAPTER XIII.

WRIT I G LETTERS.

"I hope I have not done an imprudent thing in engagin,, this young girl," thought Mrs. Thornton, laying crown her book, and gazing thoughtfully out of the window. "There seems something strange about her—something I cannot quite comprehend. If ever there was a tragedy in a human face, there is one written in hers.' Then she dismissed the subject from her thoughts. She had sent for Doris to come to her boudoir as soon as he felt herself sufficiently rested; and in a short time Doris returned to her. It was quite noticeable to Mrs. Thornton how strange and ill at ease Miss Carlisle was in her presence. "I must set her at ease," she thought. "Dear me, this shyness is most uncomfortable." So she talked easily and kindly to Doris, until the white, startled face grew calm and still. " You shall write some letters for me after luncheon, Miss Carlisle," she said. "The first, and by far the most important of them all is to my son. He left home only this moening to be gone a fortnight, and it almost seems to me that he has been gone a week." In all her life, Doris never experienced more con- flicting emotions than when she wrote that letter which Frederick's mother dictated. Iler hands trembled so over the words "My dad ling Frederick," that she could scarcely hold the pen; but with a great effort she controlled herself, knowing that the eyes of his mother were regarding her curiously. "Your hand trembles, Miss Carlisle," she said, frowning. "Are you of a nervous temperament? I have a horror of nervous people." "I am not nervous," replied Doris, in a low voice. "Oh, I hope I have not annoyed you. I I was anxious to write the letter as nicely as I could.'' "One should always take the, greatest pains with .every duty one has to perform," said Mrs. Thornton. " Well, now that you have it fairly commenced, go on to say the house seems more than lonely to me without him • that I shall expect him home sooner than a fortnight, if he can adjust matters satisfactorily. "Say that the ball which we had arranged to take place on the 20th of this month, we shall set for the eighteenth. And last—and by far the most pleasing news for him—say that Vivian, his sweet- heart, is coming to spend a few days at the villa. That will bring him home quickly, if anything can." Of course, it was only her fancy, but Mrs. Thornton quite imagined that she heard a little, gasping cry. The dark head bent lower over the white page. She could not see Miss Carlisle's face. "Heaven help me! how can I write that!" thought Doris restraining the bitter tears that threatened to fall and blot the page. "Oh, God! how can I, his wife—who love him so—write those words !" • She had not thought of facing such bitter heart pangs as this, when she entered her husband's home in disguise, accepting the position of com- panion to his haughty mother. "You write slowly, Miss Carlisle," said Mrs. Thornton, frowning impatiently. "You heard what I dictated last, did you not? The sentence was, Miss Courtney, his fiancée, is to spend a few days with us." Making a desperate effort at self-control, Doris ,penned the words, and they seemed to dance -in a mist before her eyes. She longed, with a bitter longing she could hardly repress, to cry out: "Vivian Courtney can never marry your son, cold, proud lady, for he is already wedded, and to me." What would Mrs, Thornton have said had she known that this young girl sitting so quietly by her side Was her idolised son's wife—bride to her brilliant, habdsome young heir—whom !the loved as she loved no one else on earth, and who had been parted from her bridegroom at the very altar, as it were. At last the letter was -finished, sealed and directed. Doris could have bent her head and kissed the envelope, for she knew it would rest in his hands; those dear, strong, white hands, that had thrilled her heart to the very core as they had clasped hers on that memorable wedding night, as she stood with him, so cold and awed and fright- ened, before the altar. The rest of the letters can remain until after

luncheon." Doris had not heard the remark; she was think- ing how fortunate it was that Frederick Thornton had never seen her writing. Mrs. Thornton was just about to speak again, when she, was interrupted by the sudden entrance of Isabel and Ge-endolin Thornton. Doris raised her eyes in breathless anxiety to see what Frederick's sisters were like. Cold and proud, like their haughty mother. She saw that at a glance; but despite this, her heart gave a strange thrill as Isabel Thornton glanced toward her with eyes so startlingly like her brother's. "My daughters, this is Miss Carlisle, my new - companion," said the mother, languidly. Both young ladies bowed coldly to Doris, then seemed to ignore her presence completely. Doris rose to leave the room. "You need not go," said Mrs. Thornton; and Doris resumed her seat. Their whole conversation was about the coming ball, and about their brother. • "I shall be so glad to see Frederick settled in life at last," murmured his mother. "I have al- ways been in great fear lest he should fall in love with some girl—a nobody with a pretty face and marry her dn the impulse of the moment ; he is so very impulsive." "I believe, even in that case, you would have forgiven him, mamma," laughed Gwendolin, lightly ; "you idolize him so." Mrs. Thornton drew herself up to her fullest height. "Knowing my principles as well as you do, you surprise me by uttering such a remark, Gwendolin," she said, severely. "Much as I love him, that is I the one thing that I could never have forgiven my men—marrying beneath him. I would disown him in the moment I heard of such an act of folly. He should never inherit one dollar of the Thornton estate. I would rather see him lying dead at my feet than married beneath him." "You are pleased with Vivian," smiled Gwen- " The desire ohms heart will be gratified when my son marries Vivian Courtney," replied later mother. "She is beautiful, an heires to a million. in her own right, and is a lady by birth and education. She is in every way fitted to become Frederick's bride. Why should I not be pleased'?" Doris's heart turned cold as she listened. Every word cut her heart like the thrust of a dagger. She could have cried aloud as the words fell, clear, distinct and cruel, from those cold,

- . . proud lips. She had treasured the wild hope in her poor, hungry heart that she might be able by a world of patience and endurance to win her way at last to Frederick's mother's heart; now she saw how futile had been that wild fancy. It could never be done —never while the sun shone or the grass grew. How little hope there was for her, after all. Her bowed head dropped lower and lower. Mrs. Thornton noticed it. "You look tired, Miss Carlisle," she said. " Per- haps you would like to walk out in the grounds; the air is refreshing." Doris gladly availed herself of the opportunity. Out in the solitude of the grounds, when quite shut out from the view of the open windows by the interlacing trees, Doris threw up her white arms with a bitter cry to Heaven: "Oh, I can never endure it 1" she cried. "It would drive me mad mad. I thought I was stronger, but, no, my heart is breaking. After I have looked upon Frederick's false, handsome face I will go quietly away." The words had scarcely died away on her lips ere she beheld a sight which made her catch her breath with a startled cry. A young girl in a white mull dress, carrying a white parasol in her hand edged with Oriental lace, came swiftly up the path. It was Vivian Courtney. Would Vivian recognise her, despite the dark, disguising stain on her fair face and golden hair? It was a moment of intense suspense to Doris. Her heart beat in great stifling throbs, and terror deepened in her blue eyes. With desperate hands she clung tightly to the marble surrounding the fountain. Nearer, nearer Vivian approached, eyeing in- tently the slender, girlish figure standing by the ,iouni.ain. :Caen; was something strAuxtsly id.iiiiliar

===== 0002601_18950330_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
about her, yet, looking into thd dark face, Vivian did not recognise her. With quick steps she passed Doris by and went on to the house. t' I am safe!" muttered Doris, drawing her breath hard. "She does not know me." She looked after Vivian, the graceful, haughty beauty, the belle of the seminary in that other life which seemed so far behind her, whom Frederick Thornton loved. Young, an heiress, the idol of both parents' and lover's heart—ah, what more had life to offer her! So much had been given to Vivian, while to her had been given—nothing. She asked herself why God had distributed His gifts so unfairly. Even the love that would have crowned her life and made this poor earth a heaven to her, had been taken from her and given to Vivian, the beauty. And, standing there in the red glow of the sun- light, the words of the poet came to her— "No one could tell, for nobody knew, Why love was made to gladden a few; And hearts that would forever be true Go lone and starved the whole way through:"

"But for her he might have loved me," sobbed Doris, and the pangs of jealousy, more bitter to endure than death; swept through the girl's heart. Slowly she walked back to the house, and up to Mrs. Thornton's boudoir; that lady was in the best of spirits. "I am glad you are here, Miss Carlisle," she said. "I want you to go to the apartments that have been set apart for Miss Courtney's use, and see that they are in readiness for her. See that fresh flowers a e put in the vases, and this portrait of my son Frederick placed on the mantel."

CHAPTER XIV. LIFE IS TOO HARD TO BEAR. DORIS took the portrait and turned away. Mrs. Thornton must not see the deadly pallor that stole over her as her eyes rested on that handsome face. Out in the corridor, beyond the gaze of his mother's eyes, poor Doris gave full vent to her pent-up feelings. "He was hers," she cried out, covering the por- trait with passionate kisses. "No one else had a right to love him. The picture belonged in her room—not Vivian's." Doris carried out Mrs. Thornton's instructions faithfully—all save placing Frederick Thornton's portrait on Vivian's mantel. That she carried to her own room and hid it securely from all prying eyes (never dreaming of the cruel consequences that would accrue from that rash act), there to worship it in secret. Poor Doris I Despite the fact that she believed her husband had wilfully and deliberately deserted her, she still loved him; there was something half divine in its intensity. During the next few days of Vivian's stay at Thornton Villa Doris avoided her as much as possible. A bitter pang of jealousy shot through her heart that she could neither subdue nor con- trol, as her eyes rested upon her rival. It seemed to Doris that Vivian was growing more beautiful each day. It was little wonder that Frederick Thornton loved her. Gay, piquant Vivian could count lovers by the score. They were. all so fond of Vivian at Thornton Villa it made Doris's heart ache to Eee it. She had had such wild, vain hopes of coming to Frederick's home and winning her way to the hearts of his mother and sister ? Before she had been in that aristocratic household a week she knew her dream would never be realised. One afternoon, while she was reading to Mrs. Thornton, a telegram came. "It is from my son," said his mother, laying it down with a pleased smile. " Will you oblige me, Miss Carlisle, by going to find Isabel and Vivian, and telling them Frederick will be with us this evening ? I think you will find them in Vivian's room. They were there reading not half an hour since. Why, how white you look, Miss Carlisle I Are you ill ?" she asked in wonder. "You seem dazed !" "No, no!' murmured Doris, striving to hide her intense excitement and piteous confusion ; "the room is so warm !" "And I think it extremely cold for this season of the year," replied Mrs. Thornton, im- patiently. Doris never knew how she left the room. The ceiling, the floor, the cold, proud face of Frederick's haughty mother seemed to whirl around her. She remembered nothing until she found herself in the broad corridor. She could not go to Vivian's room yet. She must sit down and try to collect her scattered senses—try to still the trembling that had seized her. Frederick was coming home. In a few short hours he would be there, and beneath his mother's roof he would meet the bride he had so cruelly deserted. Yet her disguise was so perfect he would not recognise her ; Vivian had not. "I am growing dazed, as Mrs. Thornton says," she murmured. I had almost _forgotten to deliver her message." Still trembling, she moved on down the corridor, and standing at the western window, she saw Isabel Thornton and Vivian. She walked up to them like one in a dream. Vivian turned her head haughtily, and looked at the shin, dark figure who had stopped beside her in surprise. Evidently she was not pleased with this interruption of the conversation she was having with Isabel. Then poor Doris made the first fatal mistake of her life. "Frederick is cooing here," she said. "He will be here this evennig.' The sound of her own voice seemed so hoarse and unnatural Doris could never have recognised It herself. Isabel Thornton gazed at her in astonishment, not unmixed with dismay. The familiar way in which her mother's companion spoke of her brother Frederick amazed her. In that instant Doris saw her almost fatal mis- take, and hastened to repair the great error. "These were your mother's words," she said, shrinking back confusedly. "I repeated her message in her own words.' And turning away before Isabel Thornton could find her voice, she fled down the corridor again. "A droll creature," laughed Isabel, turning to her companion. "There seems to be something so mysterious and strange about her." "I have been thinking the same thing myself," returned Vivian, with a merry laugh. "She re- minds me of someone whom I have seen; but I cannot think who, to save my life. She is pretty, too, with a strange, weird sort of gipsyish beauty. I never remember seeing before such deep blue eyes, with such a complexion and jetty hair. Do you know the fancy conies to me that I have seen that girl before 2" "She is hardly worth discussing, my dear Vivian," said Isabel, yawning, "Yet, strange to say, our Trixy is quite fond of her." That afternoon, an hour later, Doris suddenly encountered Vivian at the door of her room, as she was passing by. "I was just wishing that I could see yon," said "I have a little favour to ask of you. Would you mind coming into my room and help- ing me a little with my toilet? I—l wish to look particularly nice this afternoon." A fierce, bitter pang shot through Doris's jealous heart. She knew but too well why Vivian Court- ney desired to look so particularly nice on this eventful evening; it was because Frederick Thorn- ton was expected home. Silently she followed Vivian into her room, half a dozen of the beauty's prettiest dresses were lying across the bed; she had evidently been inspecting them. "Not one of them seems to please me to-day," declared Vivian. "I would like you to exercise your good taste by judging for me. Do you think this cream lace, with great clusters of deep-red passion roses at the belt, would be most becoming? or do you fancy I would look hest in that rose-pink mull? or the Nile green silk, with the white lace, on the back of that chair by the window?" "There seems to be little choice between the three, Miss Vivian," said Doris, in a hard, con- strained voice. "I think the cream lace will be more attractive," said Vivian, reflectively. " Will you kindly assist me a little with the looping on the left side—it has come out." Doris bowed her head and took up the dress. Ah, how beautiful Vivian would look in it she well knew. How admiringly the eyes of Frederick Thornton would rest on her—she would wear it to be attractive to him—and the thought was very bitter to Doris. How radiant Vivian looked when she stood before the mirror dressed! The cream lace dress, which showed every line of her exquisitely slender, graceful figure to the fullest advantage; the round white arms, bare to the elbows, were encircled by ruby bands that sparkled like coals of fire whichever way she turned; a necklace of rubies encircled her fair, white throat, and a dazzling butterfly, with gold and ruby wings, caught back her dark curls. Her cheeks were flushed, and her eyes glowed.

===== 0002601_18950330_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
"You have a wonderful tact tor arranging oris'e toilet, Miss Carlisle," said Vivian, critically survey- ing the effect in the mirror, and smiling—"yots have missed your vocation, 'I think. You ought ' to have been a lady's maid instead of a companion How Doris longed to turn to her and cry out: "You are mistaken. Heaven never intended me for one or the other—but for what I am—Fred- erick Thornton's wife." "I shall remember your kindness in assisting me,'. said Vivian, loftily, "and to-morrow I will look over my wardrobe and see if I can pick out some- thing!or you, Miss Carlisle ; you are very clever--; very. "Do not trouble yourself, I beg of you," said Doris, stiffly. "I would accept nothing—nothing whatever from your hands." A sudden thought seemed to strike the petted young heiress. "Perhaps you would not be averse to take money, then, Miss Carlisle. I wish to show MY appreciation in some substantial manner." Doris recoiled quickly. "No, no," she answered, huskily. accept a penny from you." And she hurriedly quitted the room ere Vivian could reply. "Was there ever such an odd, strange little creature ?" murmured the heiress. "Yet, there ill something about her that quite fascinates me in spite of myself." She went down to the drawing-room to join Isabel and Gwendolin, and thought no more of the little companion with the dark hair. Meanwhile, Doris was in her own room, sobbing as though her heart would break. How she wished that she had never come to stately, cold, proud home. It was too late to undo it now. She must live through it, let it be what it might. Oh, if Heaven would but help her! The only wish just at that moment in her heart was, that ; she could turn her face to the wall and die—dielf.' even though he would be there in a few shoril hours, sad the desire ot her heart—to look one - more on his face—be granted. She would like to kneel, with the portrait; clasped close to her heart, by her white bed Or die there. It mattered little to her what they would think or say when they came, and found oar dead. Frederick would hear of it. And perhaps )113 would come and look upon her face, understand all, and recognise her and feel sorry ; and, iu sorrow, bend and kiss her death cold. lips. Ile would know she died because she could not live without him. .

HOW HE SAVED £3O. A LEEDS MAN'S EXPERIENCE.

[FROM THE LEEDS EXPRESS.] Few people realize how much cheaper prevention of a disease is than seeking a cure. 'Mr. James Smith, of 33, Harold-street, Queen's-road, Leeds, for many years a member of one of the finest police forces ha the country, realizes it. Bet for the timely action he took in a recent emergency, he would have been at the present time a confirmed invalid, cringing at the approach of cold weather and walking abroad on the sunshiny days only by means of sticks or crutches. Long rides ;n the cold, with damp rugs for cushions, had undermined his con- stitution. A stiffeess, and :afterwards a pain, in his legs made it inconvenierr, fer him to move, and his move- ments were so painful that he was unable to work. In spite of care the pains became worse, until finally he was unable either to sit or to move about with ease. Seeing that he was getting steadily worse, he called in no less than four medical gentlemen, as he was then confined to his bed. He could not lie, sit, or stand without the most intense pain, and the footsteps of his wife or attendant on the .floor of the room caused him to writhe with agony. •The doctors informed him that he was suffering from sciatica, and that he would be a little better in warm weather. He was so, but last November the attack returned. Remedy after remedy was tried .1 hesuffered ,entold agonies. His other spell in addition to all the pain, hid cost him at least £3o,and the thought that he was about to experience a repeti- tion of all this almost drove him mad. He could mil; go upstairs on his halide and knees. However reading of them in a local newspaper, his wife advised him to try a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. He had reached that stage when he cared not what he took, and had the same lack of faith in everything. His wife having purchased a box, and one night before going through the painful ordeal of ascending the stairs, he took two of the Fills. Next morning he felt no pain while dressing, but moved very cautiously lest it should come upon him suddenly. Haying descended the stairs without any pain he gained name courage, and com- menced to walk to and fro on the kitchen floor. A few minutes afterwards his wife, to her surprise, heard him shout to her to come and see him jump over a chair. Although at 'first she could hardly credit the fact, she was forcd to the belief that her husband was reallV cured. Throughout the day he hardly felt aay pain at all, and at night upon returning horns he took two more of Dr. Williams' Pills. From that tim until the present ha has never had the slightest pain, and althoueli he has taken only two boxes of the Pills, he was able to follow his employment during the late unpreeedentedly severe weather., without the slightest fear of his old complaint. " They are only 2s. 9d. a box," said he to a representa- tive (Attie Leeds Express," and they are well worth 2s. 9d. a pill." Three or four of his friends who suffered from the same complaint,have benefited from Iris experi- ences,and wherever he goes he recommends Dr. Williams' Nair Pills for Pale People. Ihey are one of the greatest modern medicines, a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, and an unfailing cure for Influenza and its after .effects, rheumatism, sciatica, neuralgia, partial paralysis, prostration, dze. They restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex ; while in the ,cases.of men they affect a radical cure in all cases aris- ing from mental worry, overwerk, or excess. These Pills are sofa by all chemists, or post free from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 48, Holborn-viaduct, London, at 2s. 9d. a box, or six boxes for 13s. 91. Never sold loose : substitutes in this form are useless and fraudulent. The wooden box must be in s. pink wrapper with fug7 •name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION OF

BINDS.

In connection with the above association a meeting was held on Tuesday evening in the committee-room of the School Attendance Offices, the chair being men- pied by Mr. Thomas Armstrong, the band master (in the absence of Mr. T. J. Down, who had been announced to preside).

===== 0002601_18950330_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
TER ,CORPORATION SUBSCRIPTION.

The Hon. Secretary (Mr. Jwseph Taylor) said that as a result of this application the Corporation had kindly in- creased their subscription or this year from £2O to £5O. (Hear, hear, and applause.) There would be no more collecting in the park, but they could do so tor the In- firmary and any charitable object if they chose. It was ! suggested that the performances should commence on , Easter Sunday, April 14th, and finish on september 29th. There would be 55 performances at a cost of £5O 17s. The coat for the Sunday performance would be 1255. for each band, and 12s. 6d. for the week-night per- formances. There would be 15 more performances this ! year than last year. It was, he believed, intended to , straighten up OE open spaces about the town, and that performances should be giv3n at Howley and Orford Ward, although he (the speaker) believed the spaces would not be ready in time. There would, of course,be performances in the park. The public would get plenty for the L5O. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman thought the Corporation had acted handsomely in the matter. (Hear, bear.) A question arose as to the payment of bands, and Mr. Gorton contended that a band had no right to take in members outside of its dkvin band to make up the number to 25. The Chairman replied that sometimes a member of a band was sick and unable to attend, and a substitute had to be found. One or two members of the , association supported this view of the Chairmati, and it was pointed out that if substitutes were not allowed, some bands would probably be unable to give perform- ances. At the request of Mr. Gorton, the Hon. Secretary read a previous minute which had been passed to the effect that" borrowed assistance w0..41d not be recognised." Itavas then decid,ql to commence the season on Easter Sunday, the first performince to be gi veil by the Volun- teer Band. Arrangements were made as to the successive order in which other b :nds should play.

THE EXAMINER, • SATURDAY, MARCH 30, 1895.

ItUGBY.

WARRINuTON v._ LEIGH.

These two teams met on Saturday to fulfil their second engagement of the present season. The first meeting of the club took place on the first Saturday in December at Leigh, it being the first match which Leigh engaged in, after suspension. On that occasion, Leigh won by the narrow majority of a try to nil. The garneon Satur- day was consequently expected to be of a somewhat close character, and this it proved to be. Good play was, however, out of the question, fat about two hours before the time advertised for the kick off., the rain Came down in torrents,and continued during the whole of tho match. The result was that the ground was nearly satu- ated and sodden, and it was difficult to k.tep a proper foothold. Notwithstanding .these drawbacks, the game was at times very exciting, the players adapting them- selsTs to the condition, arid playing the rushing game. The Leigh team were very late in arriving, and as a re- sult,we game was commenced three-quarters ot an hour late, and only two thirties were played. Mr. Eagles, of Manchester, acted as reteree, and the following were the players comprising the two teams Warrington Boscow, full back; Barber, Moores, Heesom and Burton, three-quarters; John T. Bate and Houghton. half-hacks; Turner, Jolley, Callaghan, Saun- ders, Thorniley, Taylor, Berry and Bailey, forwards.— Leigh; Coop, full back ; Walwork,anderton, Price and Guest, three-quarters ; Officer and Taylor, half-backs ; Mac Masters, Roberts, P. Taylor, Smith, Cheetham Pemberton, France and Shaw. Turner winning the toss decided to defend the pavi- lion goal, and Warrington had during the first half the assistance of the wind. Leigh kicked off,and Houghton securing by a good run and judicious kick found touch on neutral ground. Heesom was next prominent by a break away,and Warrington quickly ecame aggressive, Coop stopping a dangerous rush of the home forwards. A mull was made in a tolerably favourable position, but Burton failed to put it over the bar, only a minor resulting. Following this let off, Officer made a good run which Burton brought to an end near the half way flag. Jolley, nowever, gained all the lost ground for • Warrington,by wriggling through his opponents,and but for Coop, the consequences would have been serious for Leigh, he causing a knock forward, and thus causing a scrimmage to be ordered. This scrimmage was decidedly in favour of Warrington, and 'Houghton getting up the ball, romped over the line. Great was the chagrin of the Warrington spectatsrs, when for some informality, the referee instead of awarding a try, only allowed a five yards' scrimmage, which Leigh screwed well and for a time averted danger. Burton a few moments later was floored on the line, and for a time the ball kept bob- bing jest on the wrong side of the Leigh line, none of the Warrington players having sufficient luck to put it over. The prolonged attack was at length repulsed and play was a little more even. Moores roused the enthu- siasm of the spectators by a fine run and equally good pass W Heesom, but the latter's course was too much blocked to be able to make much headway, and he was tackled at the quarter flag. Leigh made another spurt, and Roberts broke away in fine style and passing to Mac Masters, the latter was brought to grief by Boscow. Warrington now returned to the attack, led on by Turner. Berry and Taylor, and up to the interval, they kept measure of their opponents. It seemed as if it was to be again Warrington 'e luck to fail to score notwith- standing all their pressing. . On one occasion, the ball was taken along the goal line, almost from one side to the other, and yet no Warrington player could just put it the other side. Success, however, came to them just before the interval, and from some loose play, Callaghan smartly picked up the ball, and was over the line in a twinkling, and gained a most meritorious try. Taylor attempted to kick a goal but failed, and the whistle blowing immediately afterwards, Warrington led at the interval by three points to nil. Very little time was lost in changing sides, and Mr. Eagles had the teams at work again very quickly. Turner recommenced hostilities, and his kick off was almost nutralised by Walwork's return. Leigh commenced to take full advantage of the wind, and passing between Anderton and Walwork was the means of the latter causing great anxiety t,o Warrington, Boscow just staving off the danger by a timely tackle. Walwork again was to the fore arid was knocked into touch at the corner, the Leigh players appealing for a try, but the bail being grounded in touch, it was of course refused. A penalty kick was immediately afterwards given to Leigh in a most dangerous position. Coop took the place, but to the intense relief of the Warrington suppor.ers it went outside, arid Leigh only gained a minor. Barber at- tempted to get away after the restart, but he was too well marked, as in fact he was during the whole of the game, to become dangerous. Leigh now became the aggressors, and at length the ball was sent over the line amid a general melee. Barber rushed up and touched down, but the referee, by a doubtful decision, awarded a try to Leigh, Mac Masters being alleged to have been the, scorer. Coop took the place kick, and the ball re- bounded from the goal post. The Leigh men played with renewed vigour after this,and Bascd7r, Burt m.and Heesom were very prominent with their daring saves. The Warrington defenders were able to keep their lines intatt despiie the severe onslaught o 7 Leigh, and durisg the last five minutes the home team appeared to have got their second wind, for they forced their opponents hack to neutral territory, and were even dangerous at times. However nothing further was scored, and War- rington added one more to their long list of draws, the !game ending with the result of Warrington, one try ; and Leigh, one try.

WARRINGTON v. OLDHAM.

The match with Oldham may fairly be claimed to be the match of the season in Warrington, and conse- quently when the ground was discovered to be unfit for play ;n January on the date fixed for the return match, there was great disappointment in Warrington. The teams, however, agreed to meet on Monday, and not- withstanding the unpropitious weather, and the fact of it being a mid-week fixture, there would be fully 6,000 spectators present, a large number hailing from Oldham. It will be remembered that at the first meeting of the two clubs early in the season,a,Oldham fairly romped away with Warrington, winning to the tune of two goals, six tries to one try. Warrington were, however, doing very badly at that time, and, although they have net been exceptionally brilliant of late, a strong hope of giving a check to lie Oldliamers was held. The weather on Monday was very showery, but the field was in a much better state than i was on the previous Saturday against Leigh, and thronAout the match it kept in a state favourable to the fast play. Mr. S. Williams. of Salford, acted as referee, and the following were the players representing each team : Warrington : BOSC3W, full back ; Barber, Heesom, Moores, and Burton, three-quarters ; John T. Bite, and James T. Bate, half hacks; Turner, Jelley, Dakin, Bailey, Taylor, Thorniley, Callaghan, and Berry, for- wards. Oldham :—Eagland, full back ; Hurst,Taylor, S Lees. and Fillingham,threequarters ; Varlev and Lawton.half backs ; Bonsor, Hughes, France, Moore, Whiteley, Fur- ness, Merrell, and Newby, f4rwards. It will thus be seen that Oldham were without Mc Cutcheon and A.Lees. Turner won the toss and decided to play with the wind in the first half. Furness kicked off, and after Bi.iley's return, play was for a time in midfield, until Burton by a dashing run, put his side on the offensive, whieh was further supplemented by a dribble by Dakin and Houghton. Hurst attempted to relieve by a flying kiclr,but Boscow judiciously returned into touch. From the subsequent line out and scrim- mage,Oldharn gained some ground. but Moores,by kick- ing too strongly forced Eagland to concede the first minor. Thorniley and T ,rner led a rush from the kick out,Eagland effecting a good clearance. Varley,who up to now had been comparatively quiet, attempted to break away, but he waa -too severely tackled by Taylor to become dangerous. Another attack upon the Old- ham lines canard Eagland to kick dead for the second time. The visitors now a oke up, and some passing between the threequarters was witnessed, but Barber aveited danger, and a few moments later Warrington were almost over owing to Eagland misjudging a kick from Burton, and being tackled by Bailey before he could get it away. The submuent scrimmage, how- ever, was in favour of ,Oldham and Hurst and Taylor took the ball to the quarter flag. Here 1 urner made a good run and regained the lost ground, and Barber soon after Was almost over. Burton also had similar luck at the opposite side. A brief visit was paid by Oldham to neutral ground, but a brilliant hit nf passing between Burton. Thorniley, Heesom, and Barber, followed by a grand run by the latter, unfortunately ended in the latter being sent into touch when almost over the coveted line. The ball was sent over soon after by "Smack" Bate, but nothing was gained but a minor. Eagland mulled the return from the subsequent kick out, and just retrieved himself by kicking dead and rclobing Fhorniley in time, this being the fourth minor of the match. Oldham after almost half-au-hour's play forced their way to the home 25,but Smack Bate sent them to the right about. The Oldham backs now got to work, and some fair passing was wit- nessed between Varley, Fillingharn, Taylor, Lees, and Hurst, ended in the latter galloping over. Hurst failed at goal. Half time arrived lin media tel v afterwards with Oldham' leading by a try to nil, after Warrington having had almost all the play. Turner re-started and Merrell made his mark hot this was of little use, and Jack Bate, Barber and lletatom quickly put their side on the offensive. "Smack" Bate was only floored by Taylor on the line. A series of scrimmages took place on the line, and ' Smaek " mak- ing a sharp pass to "Jack" Bate the latter popped over the line, and thus equali,t d. J Isv made a good at- tempt at the goal. The play Was re-started with re- -1/),,t tel vigour, I.ut t ry as 0 dli iin would, they could not get past half way, fl.,scow playing a grand game, and repeatedly :ending them back. The home forwards on

oneoccasion dribbled the ball along the field, and when:a score seemed inevitale, Eaglaud dashed among them and made an A I most inirneilloua save. Tsyl.r, of War- rington, at len4t got o%er at the corner, but to the as- tonishment of the spectators, the referee only awarded a minor, alleging that Taylor was in touch in goal. Var- ley broke away soon after this let off, and passed the whole of the Warrington players except B9dCOW. That worthy however relieved the apprehensions of the home team by promptly grassing him. This was followed by a grand dribble by Callaghan which deserved a score. Moores and Jolley kept up the attack, and Oldham were over played at all points, and Taylor again got over at the opposite corner, but again, the referee would not allow a try. Oldham seamed to profit by , this, for Varley made a biilliant run to half way but his pass to Lees was knocked forward. Barber immediately afterwards got the ball,and running across the field neatly passed to Burton, and that player threaded his way through the visitors, and after a run almost half the length of the field secured a most brilli- ant try. Be failed to enhance the point, however. The lead of three points was not maintained more than three minutes, however, for Varlsy, Hurst, Tayler and Fillinghatn indulged in a brilliant bout of passiug,which ended in the latter clearing all opposition and getting over. Hurst failed at goal. Each side now endeavoured to gain tee biding point, and play fluctuated from end to end with startling rapidity. On several occasions a score seemed inevitable on both ides, but the defence on each side proved too good, and when the whistle blew, each team had scored two tries. The game was one of the most open and exciting ever seen on the War- rington ground,and the Warrington team delighted their sapporters by proving the equals, if not the superiors of tht; famous Oldham team, even in open play. • •

WARRINGTON " A " v. LEIGH " A."—The Warrington "A " team journeyed to Leigh en Saturday, and for want of a convenient train, they went by road, Messrs. Maginn providing a good 'bus. A late start was made, and it was therefore late when the team arrived at Leigh. The drive was not of a very pleasant character, rain falling nearly all the way. Rain also fell heavily during the game, and neither spectators nor players de- rived any pleasure from it, and the Leigh Club would derive very little profit, for if the " gate" came to half- a-crown that would be about all. It was twenty past four when Leigh kicked off, it having been agreed to play two thirties. Warrington returned well, and Leigh kicked into touch. Immediately afterwards they were awarded a free kick, but they secured no advan- tage, as the ball was smartly returned by Foster, and being followed up Warrington almost succeeded in scor- ing, Leigh kicking dead. For some time the visitors , pressed Leigh, and Sankey ultimately got over. Carey made a poor attempt to convert the point into a goal. , Leigh played up and tried to equalise,but the defence of the Warrington backs was too good, and they never approached within a dangerous distance of the War- rington line in the first half, at the end of which War- rington led by a try. Leigh commenced the second half very spiritedly, making the pace in fine style for ab nit a quarter of an hour, when after a series of scrimmages on the Warrington line Lloyd got over and Cunliffe placed a goal. Warrington soon showed that they had not gone to Leighto be beaten, for immediately after the restart, they entered Leigh groand; Handles secured a free kick, the ball was charged down and Randles placed it almost under the goal posts,Cross sub- sequently kicking a goal. Leigh were unable to improve their position, and the game ended in a win for the visitors by one goal one try to one goal. Owing to the state of the ground accurate play was impossible, but the score about represents the merits of the two teams, which were as follows :—Warrington: Mannion, fall- back ; Forster, Edelsten, Cross, and Sankey, three- quarters; Wood and Carey, half-backs ; Handles, Savage, Barrett, Burrows, Hughes, Thornton, Amsley and Sudlow, forwards. Leigh :—Cuncliffe, full-back ; Lloyd, Isherwood, Brown and J. Short, three-quarters ; Parry and Roberts, half-backs : Hindle,P.Shert. Taylor, Ridyard, Phcenix,Dunningliam, Mulraney and Edelston, forwards. RUNCORN v. LIVERPOOL.—At Runcorn on Saturday afternoon before a very poor gate, in consequence of the inclemency of the weather. The previous fixture was cancelled by frost. Both aides were fairly strong, Run- corn making changes in their back division, W. Faulkner and Robinson going centre threequarters. Liverpool started in a heavy rain, and for a time pressed hard,and Houghton being held on the line the latter subsequently touched down. Resuming play, J. Faulkner got off to midfield, but the visiting team again visited Houghton who kicked finely to the touch. For a lengthy time the Liverpool forwards made a strong defence. Pendlebury, the home half, repeatedly made good ground by smart kicking behind the backs, and kicking to touch. Some short passing by Runcorn, followed by a fine dribble, saw Melly making a daring save. The following pack Melly touched down. The ground had by reason of the heavy rain now become a swamp. A dribble by Walker and Pendlebury was well saved by Pearce, and Melly again distinguished himself. Play was interesting, the chief features being the very stubborn defence shown by the Liverpool men. The excitement increased as Faulk- ner and Pendlebury re-passed. W. Faulkner tackling Fraser, prevented any return. The Runcorn back divi- sion repeatedly tried to get through the Liverpool defence, but the play was very safe and all attempts to get through were neutralized. Drenching rain spoiled the play, the men hardly being able to keep their foot- ing. Half-time.—Runcorn nil ; Liverpool nil. After the interval Taylor started the ball and the home greur.d was visited. Walker dribbling back again to centre ground, the play became all forward and the backs were unable to hold the ball. The succeeding play was in the Liverpool ground, Pendlebury, Warder, and Dunba vend made repeated attacks, but the defence was strong. W. Faulkner made a determined attempt, and was held up on the line ; his passing over being thus abruptly stopped. Liverpool played well with their feet, making several ugly rushes down the ground. A strongly contested game ended. Runcorn nil, Liverpool nil.

RUNCORN V. LIVERPOOL " A" TEAMS. —ThiS'lllatCh was played at Aigburth, on Saturday, in wretched weather, rain falling heavily. The following were the teams : Runcorn : Jolley, back; Richardson, Myers, West and Gayter, three-quarter backs ; Woodward and Nickson. half backs; Stubbs, Pendlebury, Antrobus,Clarke,Shaw, Urmson, Jeacock and Brown, forwards. Liverpool: J. Frazor, hack; Jones, 'Coda, E. Frazor and Finney,three- quarter backs; O'Dwyer and A. Todd, half backs; For. wood, C. Wilson, H. Wilson, C. E. Wilson, Decker, Clarke, James and Beckett, forwards. Antrobus started and Todd failing to hold the ball, Runcorn were almost in. Runcorn shortly afterwards forced matters, and Myers receiving from Nickson eluded three or four opponents in capital style and scored behind the posts, but he failed to improve. From the drop out the Liver- pudlians were off with a big rush, and the Runcorn quarters were invaded, but their stay was only tempor- ary, as Antrobus led the way in a capital dribble, which J. Fraz,-r just managed to check by falling on the ball a yard from the line. The following pack saw Nickson rolled over when in the act of grounding. The interval arrived with Runcorn leading by a try. The second moiety was of a scrambling nature and play was chiefly confined to the forwards, back play being out of the question. Towards the close Runcorn pressed heavily, and Richardson and West scored tries but Myers failed to improve in each case. The final result was Runcorn, three tries, four miners; Liverpool, nil. WARRINGTON EXCELSIORS V. ATTIE RTON. —TDP first meeting of the above teams took place at Atherton on Saturday. Warrington commenced up hill, and by good work took play on the home lines, Mason, Long- more and Illybold being prominent. However, the weight of the forwards soon told on the visitors, and a strong rush saw Knight saving, and a minor was con- ceded. The visitors attacked in surprising fashion, and Longmore accepted an opponent's pass and dashed up the field, Houghton saving a certain score by kicking dead. This brought the interval with a clean sheet. With the slope in their favour the visitors again pressed, and Raybokl, Longmore, Smith and Mason were con- spicuous with good work. The home team now had a turn, and Knight was kept busy defending. The game still waged near the visitors' lines, and it was left to Itsybold to relieve. Securing the ball near his own lines he ran through the thick of his opponents, finishing up with a kick, and tackled the home back before he could return. Time was shortly afterwards called, the result of an even game being a draw. WARRINGTON ST. MARY'S v. WIDNES " A."—This match took place on the St. Mary's ground, Liverpool- road, on Saturday, in miserable weather, the ground being like a quagmire. Both teams were well represented The visitors won the tow,. G. Ilampson started for St. Mary's, who at once began to press, play resting at centre. Widnes were awarded a free kick, and put the ball in touch near the home 25. Widnes rushed strongly to the line, and O'Brien kicked dead. From the drop- out Murphy got possession,at 4 a zain invaded the home- sters' quarters. A lineout fol'owtd. The Saints bettered their position, Edgar taking the leather to the Widnes line. Relief came, and Geulden transferred to centre. P. liampson secured and kicked, and a well combined rush by the Saints' forwards made a severe attack upon tile Widnes defence, ansi Goulden was forced t 1 touch down. The Saints' forwards from a scrimmage as centre took the ball to the Widnes line in the corner. Ili.shfield put in a long kick to midfield, where play waged strongly until the whistle sounded half•timo,the score sheet being blank. G.Flampson commenctd hostilities with the wind and rain against the team. No return was made, and the game was evenly contested at centre. Smart play by the Saints' hacks soon eff,cted a clearance of their quarters. The forwards carried the bill on to the visitors' lines, another minor failing to their lot. Re-starting, the game was of a fast character at centre. The Widnes back division tried repeatedly to force an entrance through the Saints' defence, but the play was safe. The homesters found relief from a scrimmage. Iligginbottom I gave a long kick, Murphy secured, and passed to the visitors' right wing, and a certain score was frustrated by the accurate tackling of T. Bennat, who pulled his man down in a dangerous position. Not to be denied the Saints again returned to the attack, and a nice piece of work by the Saints' forwards took the sleather to the home lines, where they tried hard to Ferme,,Hi,,oinbottom being tackled on the line. Give and take play ensued between the respnctive hacks. Peters gave a high kick, and Roughley in attempting to catch it mulled, with the result that Grace scored ; fliglifild failed. The home- step went for it with more determination,playing resting

at centre, and from some crambling play near the line Owens got over. Higlitield larded a goal, and Widnes were declared winncrs by one goal, one try, to nil.

ASSOCIATION.

FARNWORTS,k V. ORFORD CRESCENT.—PIayed at Orford, on Saturday, in miserable weather, and on an irrigated field. Both teams played their full complement. Fern- worth started with a strong wind, and penned the home team in their defence. Half-time—Farnworth, two ; Orford Crescent, nil. Resuming play the home team 'utilized the wind to full advantage, but the visitors' defence had been strengthened by the inclusion of G. Randles. Shortly before full time Orford scored from a penalty. The remainder of the proceedings was of an Interesting character, Farnworth defending and pressing alternately. Result—Farnworth, two; Orford, one. ORFORD V. PENICETH RESERVE.—PIayed at Penketh. The visitors arrived short of three of their regular players, so played two reserves, being one man short. Orford kicked off and Penketh scored two goals in the first half. The play in the second half was mostly in favour of Orford, who played up pluckily, but as they only got the ball through once, the homesters ran out winners by 2 goals to 1. BIIRTONWOOD V. NEWTON-LE-WILLOV7S.—PIayed on the Earlestown ground. The Burtonwood team who were very heavy, did not forget to use their weight and by this means won the match, as the lighter team were by far the best footballers. Although Burtonwood won by 9 goals to 0, it was not the true state of the game for the Newton lads were often dangerous but were bowled over unmercifully time after time, and also had a very indifferent goal keeper who nearly let everything pass that came his way.

===== 0002601_18950330_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

During the present week, Warrington have engaged in I two matches,ang have added two to their long list of draws. They have surely by this time earned the title of champion drawists, for fully one third of their games this season have resulted thus. Draws are better than defeats, and the two draws this week are distinctly creditable, although in one case especially, that of the Oldham match, the result ought to have been a win for Warrington.

The match with Leigh on Saturday was a very poor game front the spectators' point of view. The past games between Leigh and Warrington have always been of the scrimmaging character, and to make things worse on Saturday, the rain commenced to fall about two hours before the match, and continued until its close. Under these conditions' the ground was little better than a puddle at the close of the game, and it is a wonder the players played so well as they did. The result of the match—a try each—fairly represents the game, for Warrington did almost all the pressing in the first half, and Leigh in the last. Callaghan scored a capital try, which is his first for Warrington. The War- rington forwards were not equal to the Leigh men, who were adepts at screwing the scrimmages. At half, the teams were fairly well matched, and " Jack " Bate did not show much sign of his long rest. Houghton was al- most übiquitous, and Warrington has ao difficulty for choice of a full back when the regular ones are absent. The threequarter rank had not over much work to do except to defend, and this they did well, being ably backed up by Boscow.

In striking contrast to the Leigh match, the match with Oldham was intensely exciting from beginning to end. It is probable that never was a more open and en- joyable match played upon the Warrington ground. Warrington showed that if they like they can meet the Oldhamers and play them a good game in their own style. The Oldham men were astonished at the result, for they came expecting to have an easy time of it. They had not forgotten the first match when they were victorious by 29 points to 3. The Warrington team, however, has undergone considerable change since then, and the " Owdamers" found these changes rather upset their calculations.

The game ended in a draw of two tries each, but this does not in the least represent the game. Warrington were pressing fully threequarters of the game,and great dissatisfaction was expressed at the referee for disallow- ing two tries which were scored by Taylor for Warring- ton. One at least was obviously a mistake of the referee. and this robbed Warrington of ''a victory which would have been well-earned. It is getting time that Warring- ton beat Oldham, for they have not done so for at least three seasons, and therefore it is all the mere annoying to miss a victory on Monday. The match should do Warrington a lot of good. They have proved that they I can play a good open game and practically beat the club which is allowed to be the leading club in this class of play. -- The game on Monday, however, showed that it is never safe for a team to relax its exertions a moment against Oldham. Previous to the last minute, Oldham were only really dangerous twice, and on each occasion scored. The two tries scored by Hurst and Fillingham were obtained by splendid bouts of passing, and showed that had they many chances of doing so, a large score would have been effected. Some pretty passing was also indulged in by Warrington, one or two bits being fully equal to Oldham. Burton's try was the best of the afternoon, for he completely dumbfounded Hurst and Eagland. They were utterly unable to stop him, and his long run roused great enthusiasm. Bate's try was obtained from a scrimmage on the line, and was by a quick drop over the line.

The Warrington forwards were the superior of the other side except in the matter of heeling out the ball. In the pack or loose they had the measure of their op- ponents. The famous Varley was well looked after by " Jack " Bate, and had not muck chance of shining, although he showed that with half a chance he would have made it hot for Warrington. " Smack " Bate had only Lawton against him,and the latter's forte appeared to be offside play. It was the excellence of the home half back which deprived the Oldham team of a lot of its sting.

The famous Oldham three-quarters found their match on Monday. Hurst could not do much, for Barber took good care of that,while on the other side Burton looked well after Fillingham, who is not much inferior to McCutcheon, for whom he played. The Oldham centres were speedier than the Warrington centres, but the latter notwithstanding, defended well, and also helped to keep up the attack. Eagland has improved a lot since the first meeting of the teams. He was almost the only weak spot at that time. On Monday he made some very plucky saves, but was not in any way equal to Boscow, who, however, had not near so much work to do. Taking the match through, Warrington had dis- tinctly hard lines in not winning outright with a good majority, and Oldham may thank their stars, and some- body else, for escaping with a draw.

To-day, Warrington will try to avenge a defeat which they received at the hands of Tyldesley. They will, however, have to fight bard to do so, and the following team has been chosen for it :—Boscow, full back ; Barber. flecsom, Moores and Burton, three-quarters ; "Jack "Bate and "Smack " Bate, half backs ; Turner, Jolley, Dakin, Taylor, Bailey, Thorniley, Callaghan and Houghton. forwards.

CYCLING.

The cycling club are already suffering from the vagaries of the gent known to the ancient Greeks as Jupiter Pluvius. They had intended to go to Knutsford for a spin on Saturday, but as it is decidedly unpleasant tn go through mud and water, they wisely resolved to stay at home. It is to be hoped that the weather this summer will be an improvement upon last. otherwise the cycling club will have to forego other rides besides Knutsford.
