IMPERIAL PARLIMENT,

FRIDAY.—THE PAYMENT OF MEMBERS. In the House of Commons on Friday, in reply to Mr. WINGFIELD DIGBY, Mr. HERBERT GARDNER stated that foreign cheese came within the scope of the Merchandise Marks Act, and if specific cases of foreign cheese being sold as English were brought to his notice he should be glad to see if the circumstances called for proceedings under the Act of last session. The debate on the Welsh Disestablishment Bill, and upon Sir M. H. Bcaca's motion for its rejection. was re- sumed,by Sir R.TEMPLE. The debate is again adjourned. At the evening sitting, on the order for going into Committee of Supply, Mr. W. ALLEN moved an amend- ment in favour of an allowance being made to members. He claimed that unless such an allowance was made they would never have in that House many able men who would be too poor to sit there without support. Another argument in favour of an allowance to members was that they would get more local men into Parliament. He strongly repudiated the suggestion that some mem- bers should be paid and some should not, and denied that it would lower the dignity of members of Parliament if they were paid. Mr. FEN- WICK, in seconding the amendment,assured the Govern- ment that in the minds of the industrial classes there was no question of ,greater importance. Sir W. Han- COURT, after some discussion, said there were too many of one class in Parliament, and, baying regard to the great extension of the suftrage, that House did not suffi- ciently represent all classes of the community.He thought the proposal was wise and expedient. The question was one of time and money. As to the money, he would be able to give them more information by and by. As to the time, the House was really more master on that point than he was. However,in timeor money he would always do what he could for the promotion of the object of the amendment. After a few words from Mr. Gos- CHEN, the House divided, and the amendment was car- ried by 176 to 158.

===== 0002601_18950330_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday, in answer to Mr. F. STEVENSON, Sir E. COLBY said the Government had no reason to suppcse that the number of Armenians confined in Turkish prisons was 3,000. Asked whether steps would be taken to prevent the rearrest of the priseners who, it was said, were about to be released by ' order of the SULTAN, he said the order for release must be held to be incompatible with any intention to have the prisoners at once rearrested. The CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER, in reply to Mr. DALZIEL, stated that although the epportunities were few he would be glad, if possible, to find an opportunity for the discussion of the interim report of the Select Committee on the Unemployed. The debate on the Welsh Disestablishment Bill was resumed by Sir G. 0. MORGAN. He said that the con- tention in the petitions sent up against the bill really amounted to this : We in England do not want dis- establishment ; therefore you in Wales shall not have it, whether you want it or not." That was a monstrous , contention. With regard to their refusal to have a religious census taken,he said that what they wanted to know was the relative proportions of the Welsh people in favour of disestablishment and azainst it, and the : best religious census for that purpose was the ballot-box. Thirty-one out of thirty-four Welsh members were re- turned as disestablishers, and the average majority of the thirty-one was 2,000. Such a record was un- paralleled in the annals of Parliamentary history. The debate was again adjourned.

===== 0002601_18950330_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.

lathe Hodse of Commons, on Tuesday, on the order for the third reading of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway Bill, Mr. DODD elicited no answer as to whether the Board of Trade had received any complaint respect- ing the hours of labour on that railway. He intimated that be would object to the order until his question was answered, and the Bill was put down for to-day. Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT informed Mr. DARLING that the Government when they considered it practicable would introduce a Bill on the subject of payment of members. _ _

A Bill introduced by Mr. S. EVANS for the enfranchise- ment of leasehold places of worship was read a first time. . _ . _

The debate on the motion for the second reading of the Established Church (Wales) Bill was resumed by Mr. D. PLUNEET, who said the framers of the Bill seemed to have studied the Irish Church Act in order to see how it was that the Irish Church had escaped its fate, and to provide that the Welsh Church should not escape in a similar way. No one who voted for this Bill could blind himself to the fact that he was in effect promoting a far larger and more invfortant measure—taking the first step towards depriving the Church of England of its endowments. Mr. G. W. RUSSELL said it seemed to him that the result of disestablishment would be to increase the spiritual strength of the church, for it was in the great towns, where the church worked on voluntary lines, that its labours were attended with the greatest success, and excited the greatest attachment. In his opinion, the main losses which the church would suffer from disestablishment would be that the bishops would no longer sit in the House of Lords, and that presenta- tions to livings would no longer be vested in the hands of patrons. Lord WOLMER said the effect of the Bill, it it became law, would be to dislocate the working of the whole machinery of the Church in Wales. Mr. ALFRED THOMAS hoped the Bill would become law. Mr. STOCK opposed the second reading of a Bill of which sacrileg:- and spoliation were part and parcel. The debate was continued by Sir S. Morcreuti, Mr. J. A. CAMPBELL, Mr. WALLACE, Sir H. S. ICING, Mr. A. J. WILLIAMS, Mr. GRIFFITII-ROSCAWEN, and Mr. LLOFD•GBORGE.

WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday, the Lanca- shire and Yorkshire Railway Bill was ordered to be read a third time. Mr. James O'CoNwoa moved the second reading of the Irish Lights Bill, which, be said, proposed such a reconstruction of the Irish Lights Board as would make it representative of the interests of the various seaports of Ireland. The second reading was rejected by 193 to 76. _

Mr. SEYMOUR KEAY moved the second reading of the Farm Servants (Scotland) Bill, which, he said, was intended to remove old and admitted grievances of farm servants in Scotland in regard to their housing. Sir WILLIAM WEDDERBURN seconded the motion, and after some debate the bill was read a second time. Mr. Bryce obtained leave to introduce a bill to amend the Fisheries Acts. •

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

The disagreeable weather of Sunday had very little effect on the attendance at the r.s.e. meeting at Wy- cliffe Church,where the usual routine was gone through, with Councillor A. Bennett in the chair. In his opening address the Chairman gave a brief summary of the I work of the P.S.A. movement, but the speaker proper was Mr.Standfast.of Chorlton•cum-Hardy.who.however, only " stood fast" for about ten minutes, though his discourse in that time was of the best, being on the subject, " Where art Thou ?" Mr. F. Fieldhouse was the soloist, and his song, "There is a green hilt" was delivered with good effect, which was not lost on the audience. Tke usual Scripture lesson was read by Mr. James Brown. SEKVICES FOR THE PEOPLE.

The usual people's service was held in Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday afternoon, and was con- ducted by the Rev. M. Hayward, ot Rochdale. Mr. F. 0. Moston, of Lymm, gave the address. His subject, was Christ's standard of life, which was embodied in the words "Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul and with all thy mind, and with all thy strength, and thy neighbour as thyself." , This meant that they must have whole-hearted love to I God and unwavering sympathy fir their fellowmen. Fruit was the standard to which every true soldier must attain. It was a high standard, but nevertheless a possible one. Jesus Christ Himself had lived it, Paul had lived it, and men of their own day, snch as Wesley and Wilbeeorce, had lived it. There must be no retreat; their motto must be. " Advance." This standard of life, he said, was not only possible, but practical. It was their duty to consider every plan and every question which presented itself or was presented to them. They must not despise and refuse it, but they must measure It by the standard of Jesus Christ. If it were a worthy plan let them consider it, let them be in sympathy with it, let them help it, let them join it, let them pray for it, and thus they would be helping to make the world better for having lived in it. Several selections of music were well rendered by the orchestra, and a duet was sung by Miss Sherlock and Miss Tunnicliffe.

FRODSHAIif.

ASSAULT ON THE POLICE—On Saturday evening the peace and quietness of Main-street Frodshain was con- siderably disturbed bp the conduct of a number of Nor- wsgian sailors, hailing from the ship Asia, lying at Salt- port. On the police remonstrating with them, Police Constable Price was injured on his head and face. At Frodshain Police Court the five sailors whose names were Hans Pederson, Karl Hanson, Albert Nelson, John Halvorsen, and august& K,rlander, were brought before the magistrates on Monday. charged with assaulting Sergeant Hunt and P.C. I'rice. The evidence was that at ten o'clock on Sunday night the officers mentioned were on duty in Main-st., nodal:tin, when they liiard two men making a noise. They spoke to them, and then the men went back and were joined by four more sailors. They proceeded into liooth-st. where they collected stones, and then threw them n at the police. The ser- geant was struck on the belt, but P.C. Price fared worse, he being hit in the ja w and stunned for a time. The policemen afternards had to fight their we v, getting into close quarters, and were fbrced to use their sticks. The sailors hastily departed, and the °dicers crossed Some fields and intercepted them and were able to bring Nielson to the police station, and he was placed in the cells. Having obtained the assistance of seven other constables they prooeeded to Saltport and took the other men into custody.—nr. Selby gave particulars Of the injuries to the Constable's chin, stating that if the blow had been received in Me centre of the jaw it would have caused a compound tracture. The prisoners were each fined £2 including costs.

GLADiTONB BAG (Cow Hide) From 16s. LADIES DRESS BASKETS From 245. WATERPROOF HOLD-ALI From 9s. 6d 39 AND 411 DALE STREET. 125 LORD STREET, AND 84 PARKER STREET, LIVERPOOL.

MR. MATHER, M.P., whose inter- A Sugg,estive esting and valuable experiments Scheme.

with regard to the effects of an eight hours day in the engineering trade did so much a few months back to help forward the cause of shorter hours of labour, has again done excellent service to the community by the scheme for dealing with the unemployed which he has put forward. MrVAranne is a member of the Select Committee of the House of Commons which is inquiring into the contest and remedies of unem- ployment, hut he is not one of the members who are content to simply listen to or criticise the pro- posals which are made by others. He has prepared a plan of his own which he subrriits to the con- sideration of the Committee and of the whole country. Without dealing with the rather elabo- rate details of the scheme, which, almost of neces- sity,will need to be perfected by experience,we cer- tainly think that Mr. MATHER is cm the right line with respect to his main proposals. For one thing, he does not make the elistake'of under estimating the gravity of the unemployment problem. He is evidently not of the opinion that the causes which are depriving so many thousands of work- men of their means of livelihood are of a mere transient character, and that, therefore, the evil which results may be tided over by temporary grants from public funds or an increase in private charity. The lack of employment is a steadily growing evil, and it calls for a permanent remedy. The time has really gone by for mere task work expedients, such as stone-breaking and oakum- picking, which are comparatively unproductive and often degrading to those who are put to them. As Mr. MATHER in effect points out, what is now required is that the superfluous labour should be brought into contact •with " the re- sources of nature hitherto unfruitful, without in- terfering with the labour of any other men, or wasting the capital required for the purpose; and thereby enriching in some degree the whole nation, as well as providing relief from an ever- growing social danger and industrial burden." In other words, Mr. MATHER'S scheme is to utilise idle hands to cultivate and develop the resources of idle, or comparatively idle, land. The scheme is divided into two parts, so as to deal with the two different classes of unemployed—the first class being the temporarily unemployed, and the second class those who have been absolutely dis- placed from the industries they had hitherto fol- lowed and who must therefore seek out a new way of living. He suggests that the first class should he temporarily provided for in 1" farm training colonies," started by public money and under public control. He concludes from the experience that has been gained in similar colonies in Germany and France and nearer home still at the Salvation Army Colony, that I the labour of any able-bodied man upon laud can, under proper directions and conditions, be of at least an equal value to the cost of his maintenance. These colonies would, Mr. MATHER maintains, be not only self- supporting, hut would enhance the value of the land upon which they were founded and therefore add to the productive resources of the nation. 'There will," he says, "be a constantly growing increment in the value of the land, die to the labour of those employed upon it; and that incre- ment will in itself—apart from any consideration of the use of the land for the purpose of meeting and dealing with a great social difficulty—accrue to the State as a valuable asset to set off against any loss of interest during the years when the land is being used as a training colony, and before the period when it will have become an interest-bear- ing investment." In the second part of his scheme Mr. MATHER provides for those who are under a necessity to seek a fresh source of livelihood from the land. For these he would provide "small farm settlements" through the medium of County

Councils and upon conditions which he enumer- ates at considerable length. This too; he con- siders, would when conducted by the State be a paying specu:ation even in regard to its finances alone. As to whether men could be found in sufficient numbers to make the experiment feasible he believes that the evidence already given before the Parliamentary Committee has answered that in the affirmative. Then, of course, with respect to the objections that the land of England will not support a largely increased number of persons upon it, and that families could not derive a living from smallholdings of eight acres each, there are ready and conclusive replies. Within the last twenty years alone nearly two million acres of arable land has been laid down to grass and portions of this or similar land might easily be obtained in diffet- ent parts of the country upon which to test the proposed scheme. For the objection that a living could not be made on those small holdings there is even less to be said, so long as something of a co-operative character is given to the settlements. By what is known as " intensive " cultivation,which seems to be much better understood in some parts of the Continent than in England,families are eaabled, where there is a free exchange for produoe, to live on a much less plot of land than eight acres:; and as an additional guarantee the farm settlements would under the present proposals be placed under the same skilled general management and supervision as the farm colonies. In fact,taking it as a whole Mr. MATHER'S scheme is the most adequate and helpful which has yet been placed before the Unemployed Committee. It will doubtless be severely criticised and probably ridiculed in sane quarters, but under the circumstances in which a large number of the industrial population of the country are at present placed something more than criticism and ridicule is required. If the critics have a more feasible plan for dealing with an admitted evil than that proposed by gr. MATHER no it is the time to produce it.

THEE is a marked absence of Disunited Unionists. unity amongst the Unionists at the present time. At Leamington' from the representation of which the present Speaker of the House of Commons) is about to retire, there are two so-called " Unionist "candidates in the field—one a Tory and the other*a Liberal Unionist, the son of the Speaker. As the Speaker is a Liberal Unionist that party claims that his successor shall also be a Liberal Unionist according to 3 bargain which they say was made in 1886. Tbe Tories, however, show a strong desire to repudiate the agreement, and have nominated a candidate

of their own, and this candidate has hithero emphatically declined to retire from the field spite of the urgent complaints and entreaties of the Liberal Unionist leaders. In another dire& tion the "union of the Unionists" has bee° seriously shaken. Mr. COURTNEY is a° ethinent Liberal Unionist member of th° House of Commons, and no its boil' announced that there would soon be a vacancy ill

the Speakership there seemed to be a general coo' census of opinion that he was the most suitable man to succeed Mr. PEEL. But the whole TorY party unanimously revolted against al such proposal. So far as they were concerne. ' they would rather have a Radical in the ellair than Mr. COURTNZY, and that gentleman has consequence been thrown over even by his °via party. These are only two of the accumulating symptoms that the:alliance between the two wlog9 of the Unionists is on the verge of dissolution.. The mutual recriminations that are indulged °I are also in the highest degree suggestive. On the one hand the Liberal Unionists are bluntly told by their good friends, the Tories, that they er° 114, constantly diminishing quantity, and that aPar" from the Birmingham district their numbers are infinitesimal. On the other hand the Tories are assured that if once the bandof union is broke° they will lose the support, not only of the mingham contingent, but also several seats I° different parts of the country which are at Pre' sent held by small majorities. The quarrel is very " pretty one" as it stands, and can hardli fail to nave important results in the immediate future.

There was a great diversity of opinion among the Warrington Guardians at their meeting on Wed- nesday, last with regard to the various tenders for the ensuing half-year, which bad to be accepted on that day. There was scarcely a single tender ac- cepted without either a division or a protest being made. A very commendable desire was evinced by some of the Guardians that everything should be

done by the open Board. Mr. Knowles, however, was rather inconsistent. At the commencement he Was the champion for having things done by the Whole Board and not by committees, and spoke strongly in favour of the former, and yet he sup- ported a proposal to refer the coal tender to the Rouse Committee. Be admitted that he was incon- sistent in so doing,but maintained that there was a difference 'between the coal tenders and those for Rroceries and drapery. He did not explain what was the difference, however.

It is very evident that no stranger need tender 40 the Board. In the meat tenders, the man who bad been in the town the longest, although he did' _ll°L send in the lowest tender, was the successful 'lle, while it was actually proposed not to consider ?lie of the tenders for leather because it was sent 14 by a London firm. It is very evident that the resent Board of Guardians is determined that local tradesmen shall profit by the tenders, and not "foreigners."

d Tb'e Ministers of the Warrington district are evi- °'Y awakening, and endeavouring to get an in- amount of power. Matthews, and Mitchell—de- One of these three, the Rev. T. R.Matthews, veek ok Bince been elected at Earlestown, and last to his seat for the first time on the Board Guardians. RUNCORN 'AND WIDNES.

THE GALE AT RUNCORN. A very severe storm prevailed in Runcorn, Widrue and neighbourhood en Sunday, but no damage of any moment was done. The wind was very high at different times during the day, and at intervals there were heavy showers of rain and sleet.

DRUNKENNESS.

On Wednesday mornla;,—;El:Corn Police Court, be- fore Mr. Imison, John McNatty was fined ss. for bein_ drunk on the 26th inst. ; and oa Thursday morning at the same court, John Budd, boatman. Droylsden, was fined Ss. and costs for being drunk and disorderly in Bridge-street, the previous night. Si. PAUL'S BIBLE CLASS, RUNCORN. Mr. B. Kniveton, the president, gave a Bible lesson at the meeting of this class on Sunday. Solos were pleas- ingly rendered by Mr. Charles Atherton and Mr. William Gowanloch, and Mr. William Cooke played a solo on the Organ. SONS OF TEMPERANCE, RUNCORN. Mr. John Jones presided at the usual meeting of the above order on Saturday. An admirable paper was read by Mr. David Smith on the "Brotherhood of Man." The writer spoke of their common parentage, and pointed , out that God was the great father of them all. He I showed that if men only adhered to the golden rule they would have better social conditions and a more whole- some public life. A discussion followed, taken part in by Messrs. E. Key, D. Bisbrown, S. M. Temperley,James J. Holt, James Williamson and George Holt. A vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Smith for his paper.

RUNCORN FRF:E LIBRARY: - Mr. T. %Bey presided at the meeting of the Library Committee en Tuesday evening. Mr. Jones (librarian) read his monthly statement, from which it appears that the number of books issued during the month of Febru- ary was 2,088, being a decrease of 151 in the lending department, and nine in the reference department. The books issued in the former department are classified as follows: Theology 35, philosophy 13, science 79, history 151, poetry 19, fiction 1,560, miscellaneous 178. In the reference department 53 books were issued. The follow- ing gifts were acknowledged: Sunday magazine (2 vols.) from Mr. J. E. Langshaw ; catalogue of snakes (vol. 2, 1894.) from tru,tees of the British Museum; and also catalogue of mycetozoa (1894). Free library reports were also received from Liverpool and Widnes. SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RUNCORN. There was a large attendance at the weekly entertain- ment provided at this Institute on Tuesday evening.Mr, W. Shaw, missionary, presided, and referred to the ob- lject of which the proceeds were in aid of, viz. : The Isailors grave and monument fund. The programme in- cluded songs by Miss Randles, Miss Shallcross, Miss Douglas, Messrs. T. Shallcross, W. Cooper, W. Marsh, P. ' Green, J. Cliffe, and T. Shaw. Recitations were given , by Mr.' W. Hulse, and Mr. Byfield contributed a humor- ous reading. The band under the conductorship of Mr. R. Clays rendered several selections. The accompanists were Miss M. Rowyer and Mr. .1. Foulkes. BRUNSWICK WESLEYAN BAND OF HOPE. Mr. T. Lea presided at a meeting of this Band of Hope lon Monday evening, when there was a large attendance. An interesting programme was submitted and well gone through. The choir rendered glees; and a number of recitations were given by the following :—Lucy Deakin, Gerty Coventry, Martha Smitham Mary Clarke, Helena Smitham, Leonard Ellison, and John Bisbrown. Songs were sung by Miss Leathwood, Miss Jeffrey, and Miss Maggie Hill. Miss Smith nicely rendered a violin solo, and an amusing dialogue was taken part in by a number of noys and girls. Mr. Alfred Norman delivered a short ' address, as did also the Chairman. Mr. John Bisbrown was the accompanist. CADETS OF TEMPERANCE, RUNCORN. A meeting of this order was held on Saturday, night under the presidency of Master HerlArt Andrews. An interesting addl.( es was delivered by Mr. James Whyte. Mr. Thomas Cook presented a book entitled " Hardy land Fool-Hardy "to Master Thomas W. Whyte for secur- ing the largest number of members during the quarter. The prize was given by Mr. Thomas J. Holt. Mr. Cooke said the future of England depended on the proper train- ing of the young. If they were to have a sober nation, the young must avoid drink. He gave some examples of the havoc wrought by intemperance among educated people. He also urged the importance of personal influ- ence. In conclusion Mr. Cooke said he would offer two prizes to the boys who should get the largest number to ' join the order during the next quarter. Mr. Daniel Big- brown, and Mr. Edward Key took part in the meeting. A vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Cooke on the pro- position of Mr. James Loft. PRESENTATION TO TUB REV. J. HOLDEN, RUNCORN. ANOTHER DROWNING MYSTERY NEAR RUNCORN.

On Thursday morning, about seven o'clock, whilst Thomas Lydiate, of Runcorn, was working on the dred- ger Irk, in the Ship Canal, near Weston Point Church,he saw the body of a man in one of the wells. He and an- other person fastened a :rope to it, and sent for P.C. Hammersley, who took' charge of it, and removed it to the Weaver Hotel, where it remains awaiting the inquest. The body was far advanced in decomposition, and the clothing very much torn and disarranged. The pockets contained 7d.—During the day John Wilkinson identified the body as that of John Williams, aged 45 years, flat- man, belonging to Northwich. Be had known him for 15 years. The deceased, on December 29th last, visited the Weaver Hotel, and left there at 10 o'clock. The land- lord, Mr. Coyle, states that he was quite sober, and on leaving promised to come early next morning to clean out the stables. It will be remembered that on the same date, Mr. Woodhouse, captain of the dredger Barry, was also missed, and his body was not found until a fort- night ago. At the inquest upon him it was shown that the night of December 290 was very boisterous, and that several very heavy hailstorms were experienced. How the man got into the canal is a mystery.

ASTON FANMERS AND TIME MILL At the Widnes Petty Sessionson Thursday, before Mr. E. K. Muspratt (in the chair) and Mr. S. Sadler, George and James Percival, Park Farm, Aston, Cheshire, were summoned for selling a quantity if milk to P.S..Bennett, of the Widnes police force, which was not of the sub- stance and isature demanded by the purchaser. Henry Stanley, Farnworth, was also summoned for selling the milk. Mr. G. F. Allen (St. Helens) appeared for the defendants.—P.S. Bennett, in giving evidence, stated that on the 16th March he purchased a quart of new , milk from the man Stanley, whe was selling milk from a cart, in the use of the defendants. He divided it in the office,and the defendant produced a paper on which was written" Cheap new milk, guaranteed ten per cent. milk, sold at 2d. per quart." The notice on the cart was " Milk solids ten per cent." Stanley tald him he was selling it on behalf of George Percival,f armer, Aston, Cheshire.—Superintendent Keighley read the report from the county analyst •f Cheshire, in which it was stated that the milk, as sent by P.S. Bennett, contained 1.29 per cent. of fat, nine per cent. of other solids, total 10.29 per cent. of solids. It had been deprived of half of its cream.—By Mr. Lewin ; The usual per cent. was three per cent.—Mr. Allen, for the defence, said his clients had not sold milk to the prejudice of the public. They sold it as new milk,which indeed it was. The re- port stated it contained 10.29 per cent, of total solids.— The magistrates having conferred together for a few moments, the Chairman intimated that the defendant ' George Percival would be fined LI. and .£1 is. Bd.costs ; James Percival, 83. 61. ; and Stanley 51. and 12s. costs. CAMDEN WESLEYAN SCHOOL RUNCORN. On Thursday evening, a well-attended gathering was held in this school, under the auspices of the Girls's Parlour. The chair was occupied by Mr. James Taylor. An interesting programme was submitted, consisting of vocal and instrumental music, with scenic effects. The illustrations were provided by means of a powerful limelight, which was manipulated by Mr. Robinson. ST. JOHN AMBULANCE ASSOCIATIOLRUNCORN BRANCH. The annual meeting of the members of this society was held on Thursday evening, in the Board Schools, under the presidency of Mr. F. J. Norman. J.P. There was a fair attendance, including Dr. McDougall.—Mr. I Clare, the secretary, submitted his annual report, in which it was stated that, great good had been done by the lectures in the past,for in consequence of the valuable teachings of their hon. lecturer considerable over 300 persons had gained certificates, and many more attended who did not present themselves at the examination. Members during the past year the members had acquitted themselves credititly in cases whieh came und.er their notice not only in Runcorn, but outside. The work of the past year had been a little disappointing through the dilatory attendance of the members, and owing to many leaving the town. Mr. Epps, secretary ; Mr. Rhodes, secretary ; Mr. Henderson,treasurer ; Sergeants Woolley and Benfield, members of the committee, had recently remeved elsewhere. An ambulance conveyance had been- purchased. and might be used by any member of the brigade. At the recent examination held by Dr. Morgan, of "Liverpool, every person who presented himself passed thus proving the stability af the teaching given by Dr. McDougall. Mr. Clare also read the financial statement, which showed that the year commenced with a balance of £lO 2s. 21., the subscriptions realised £8 18s., and sundries made the income into £23 93. NI The expen- diture showed that £l3 17s. 11d. had to bo paid for the ambulance litter, and after meeting all other liabilities there was a balance in hand of £1 175.7-il. The accounts were made up to November 30th, 1894.—The report and balance sheet were adopted, on the motion of Mr. New- ton, seconded by Mr. D. Smith. Time election of officers also took place. Mr. Brundrit, J.P., was re-elected pre- sident ; the list of vicepresidents WNS passed ; Mr. Clare was chosen secretary ; and Mr. Bankes was appointed treasurer. Votes of thanks were accor h d to the late secretary and treasurer. The Committee was also elected.

THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY. MARCH 30, 1895.

FVNE@AL OF A WIDNESIAN.

On Thursday- afternoon- the remains of the late Mr. Martin Maloney (46), who died at his residence in Major Cross-street, were interred at St. Bede's, Appleton. De- ceased had for some time been a foreman at Muspratt's Chemical Works, but left a few ye ir: go, and went to Liverpool, where be started business. He came back to Widnes recently, but caught a cold, from the effects of which he died as stated. Two coaches containing the re- latives of the deceased followed the hearse to the place of interment, where the last rites were performed by Father North. A large number of the employes of the Alkali Works walked in front of the hearse,and attended the service. The funeral arrangements were in the hands of Mr. J. H. Lee, of Widnes.

A TESTIMONIAL FOR VIE SURVEYOR.

Mr. Swinton, the surveyor, then applied Mr a testi- monial as to his services, from the Board.—Mr. Long said Mr. Swinton had served them well, and they could not refuse a request of that sort, when it would help him on his way.—Mr. Dutton then moved the sanction of the Board to the request, and on Mr. Tickle seconding it, it was carried and the clerk instructed to write out a testi- monial.

TOTES OP THANKS. Mr. Long did not think they could part as a Board without regarding the services of the Chairman, and al- though he did not think that Mr. Burgess was in any need of a testimonial from them, still they could not allow a parting without expressing that sense of their obligation to him for the kind and courteous manner in which he had tiled the offices of chairman and vice-chairman. He moved that the thanks of the Board be given to Mr. Burgess for his services in the past.—Mr. James Parr seconded, and said that he had known Mr. Burgess for some time, and he was sure the chairman had the good wishes and respect ot everybody, for he hid treated both high and low with great courtesy and respect.— Mr. Barber in supporting the resolution, added that Mr. Burgess had ben one of themselves as much as it was possible for a chairman:to be, and he was in touch with all the business .f the Board.—ln respond ing,M r. nurgess said it had given him great pleasure to have a tote of thanks accorded to Var. There had been many changes on that Board, and he did not think there was a single man living who was connected with the first Board 32 years ago.—lt was mentioned, however,that there were several ex-officio members. . .

Mr. Burgess then proposed a vote of thanks to their vice-chairman, who he believed was the oldest member, and who was unfortunately away ill.—Mr. Tickle :ee- condetJ. and the vote was carried. The Cleric thanked the Board for their courtesy to- wards himself in his capacity as Clerk, and Mr. Long in replying said Mr. Linaker wee highly respected among them, and was a man:who had always done his duty.— This was all the business.

After the meeting the members were treated to a sub- stantial and appetising repast in the 'Ring-o'-Bells Inn," in catering for which the landlord gave univeral satis- faction.

TOM MANN AT 10INCORN.

CHRISTIANITY AND SOCIALISM.

In the Salvation Army Hall, Runcorn, on Wednesday evening, there was a large attendance at a meeting held under the auspices of the Runcorn branch of the Inde- pendent Labour Party. The chief speaker was Mr. Tom Mann, who spoke en the subject, "Socialism and its relation to Christianity." The chair was occupied by Comrade Sexton,of Liverpool,and there were also on the platform i—Messrs. H. Stanley, J. Peters, a Chid, DR. Callow (Widnes), W. Wakefield (Widnes), and J. Pegg. After a short address from the Chairmanr

Mr. Torn Mann, who met with a most hearty recep- tion, said it was for them to consider whether or no their present civilization was satisfactory. He might say that they had no policy of revenge, no policy of hatred, but only a policy of love. According ' to their principles they tried to understand what was right, to love it, and therefore to live it. (Applau,e.) They had come to this conclusion that right relation- ' ships between citizen and citizen were absolutely neces- sary if righteousness was to prevail. There must be no exploitation of one section of the community by another section. (Hear, hear.) If they believed in righteousness they could not believe at the same time in robbery, even if that robbery be legalised. Because they believed in right doing they could not believe at the same time in the strong, living at the expense of the weak, even if the customs of English society sanctioned it ; and because they believed in right doing they dare not subscribe Co the existing institutions which called for the industrial and social discord that prevailed to- day. He could deal with any of the staple indus- tries in the country, and could show the terrible conflicts that were going on. On the surface they had now the boot and shoe lockout. What was the cause? It was deeper than mere antagonism between operator and employer. There was a root cause for these indus- trial difficultjes. They could go all round the country and find that discord and not harmony obtained. They had in Great Britain no fewer than one and a half mil- lions of men in enforced idleness. It was not because they were lazy. but because they were unable to find an eptlet for their energy. This meant that no less than five millions of men women and children in Great Brit- ain to-day were suffering acutely because the principals of the families were unable to find employment. When they dealt with those who were only partially employed and those who were employed irregularly they would touch one third of the total population. He would ask what did they understand by Christianity, should be done? Religion did not consist in going to church or chapel, reading the bible, singing hymns, or in joining in prayers. That which passed for religion in the aver- age church and average chapel, that which was put forth in the ordinary bible class, in the ordinary Sunday school was not only not religious but it was distinctly irreligious and demoralising. (Applause.) He knew he was speaking to men connected• with the chapels and churches in the district. Religion consisted in revolting against wrong-doing and seeking to establish right. doing. If they had regard for religion they had to have common regard for the common brotherhood and sister- hood. That was their doctrine. They adhered to the old doctrine that the brotherhood and sisterhood of humanity must be their brotherhood and sisterhood; that it must be real, and to have it real it would never attempt to exploit from one section of the community at another's expense. (Applause.) In this country did their chapels and churches preach such a doctrine? No, , they did not. He was prepared to give the lie to anyone who would dare to say they did. There might be. a ,few exceptions, but exceptions only proved the rule. Where were the ministers of the gospel in that district, in that county, or in Britain that dared declare against usury which was not only condemned by Christ, but by the Jews before Christ's time ? The congregations prayed "Thy will be done in heaven,, so upon the earth,' and yet the cause of the pernicious industrial and social system that obtained to-day was buttressed up, main- tained, and defended by those who composed the legis- lature, those who composed the political parties, and those who belonged to their orthodox iustitutions. (Applause.) The speaker then proceeded to speak of the conditions under which the people lived ; and referred to the long hours worked by some, contrasting it with those who were unable to work. He asked what spirituality- could be developed by a roan who worked an 84 hour week all the year round. Do not let them insult religion by acquiescing in such conditions. If they asked why the churches had lost hold on the work- ing classes, there they had the answer. They ought not to be content with this living death ; let theni yearn after real life. Life was what they wanted. A number of questions were afterwards asked, and answered by Mr. Mann. The meeting concluded with cheers for the chairman and speaker.

===== 0002601_18950330_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
DARESBU Y HIGHWAY BOARD.

FINAL MEETING. A meeting of the above Board was held in the Board room, Daresbury, on Thursday afternoon, under the presidency of Mr. T. S. Burgos. and there were also present Messrs. William Long, Darbyshire, J. Darlington, H. Eyes, J. Barber, T. Lea, J. Parr, P. fickle, W. Beswick, T. Dutton, H. J. Linaker (clerk), and '1". Swinton, (surveyor). After the Clerk had read the minutes of the last meet- ing, which were unanimously passed, Mr. Darlington proposed that a vote of sympathy be forwarded to Mr. Derbyshire in his sad bereavement, he having lost his son. The vote was seconded and after Mr. Long had passed a few remarks sympathising with Mr.Darbyshire, as he had had a personal knowledge of his son,the motion was passed. The Olerk read a letter from the Local Government Board, enclosing a cirsular letter to the Warrington Cor- porationsayingsayin that they had ordered an enquiry to be held on the 2nd rof April, with reference to the extension of the borough of Warrington. This was not of any soment to them, however, as they would be out of office before that date. DANGERS FROM DFIFECTIVE ROAD.

A letter was also read from Mr. .Thomas, of Rancorn, drawing the attention of the Board to the Stag Inn swing bridge, which he said was so unevenly fitted with the road that he had bad the shoes of his horse .pulled off, and he observed that if the shoes had not come off it was possible that the horse would have been thrown down. He wished to know from whom he should claim damages in the event of a casualty—the Ship Canal Company, or the Highway Board ?—The Clerk stated that he had mentioned the matter to Sir E. Leader Williams, and had received a reply stating that that gen- tleman would ,ace that the matter was rut right.—Mr. Long said that as they would terminate office after that meeting, the District Council had better be informed of the matter.—The Clerk promised to pass on this recom- merwiation to the Ship Canal Company, and to Mr. Thanes. Mr. Linaker also read a communication from Messrs. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

The Ship Canal negotiations with Runcorn, rela- tive to that now hackneyed teim—the graving dock question—are now practically completed, and Runcorn comes out of them with decided advan- vantages. A deputation of the Runcorn Council attended at the Manchester Ship Canal Company's offices on Monday, and from their report submitted at a special meeting of the Urban Council on Tues- day night, it appears that the Canal Company at once declined to accept the proposed condition of the Council for a ten years' option of establishing a graving dock on the west side of the railway bridge, on the ground that it would interfere with the development of the Company's docks, etc. But whilst they raised this objection, after a little pressing, they acceded to a request to waive the suspension of the graving dock clause for 20 or 25 years, instead of rescinding it altogether.

Another satisfactory feature of the report is that the Company have decided to provide still further lay-by accommodation, in all 1,300 feet, and in this respect it seems as if the Company have now ar- rived at the rational conclusion that it will be better to earn a dividend through inducing large vessels to come along the Ship Canal to Runcorn, than perhaps not getting them to enter it at all, owing to the strong aversion of some ship owners to cut their ships' masts in order to get under the bridges in the journey to Manchester. Of course this would be unnecessary in coming to Runcorn, as there would be io bridges to pass.

Other advantages promised in the agreement in- clude the provision of coal and salt tips, and these, considering the position of Runcorn should prove beneficial to the trade of the town. The opposition used on the last occasion when the terms were dis- cussed by the Council, melted away on Tussday evening, and this was perhaps due to the Canal Co., deciding not to waive entirely their obligation to o-nstruct a graving dock. Only one member of the Council—Mr. Kirkham—refrained from voting, and the remainder were In favour of the Commit- tee's proposal to accept the terms come to at Man- chester on Monday.

Now that the negotiations may be said to be con- cluded, the people of Runcorn will be anxious to see the agreemea put into execution. It is gratify- ing to learn from the deputation who visited Man- chester, that Mr. Bythell is making arrangements for the work to be proceeded with at once. This will be good news to the unemployed men in Runcorn.

The letters which were read at the meeting of the Halton Parish Council on Tuesday night relat- ing to the repair of roads and footpaths in the dis- trict, would be a sore disappointment to those who had thought that the Council's recommendations for their repair would be carried out. The Council seem, to use the words of one of the members, to have gone "out of their depth " in looking after the repair of roads., for it is purely a matter for the District Council. The only work in this direction for the Parish Council is the care of right-of-ways, which they may acquire by agreement. The Coun- cil ultimately decided that a schedule should be drawn up,mentioning the roads reqairing attention for submission to the District Council. This is practically all that the Parish Council are able to do in the matter.

The visit of Tom Mann to Runcorn was an event 'which has been looked forward to with a greet deal of interest by local "1. L. Peers." They were con- sidered fortunate in being able to get him to Run- corn, and they took occasion to do their utmost to make the visit of a profitable nature, for in addi- tion to charging for admission, a collection was also taken up. Mr. Mann's address. was well thought out, and was delivered with his usual ability. lie condemned in no measured terms what passed for religion in the present day, saying that "religion consisted in revolting against wrong doing and seeking to establish right doing." There were, of course, the usual stiong epithets against interest and dividends, one word " exploiting" being very conspicuous. lie certainly gave his hearers some- thing to think about, but whether the great changes which he prognosticated will take place within ten years, is a matter of speculation.

ANOTHER STEAMER AGROUND IN THE 6111 P CANAL.

This (Friday) morning at ten o'clock a large steamer, City of Dublin, r.in aground at the bend at Runcorn. She is 361Ifeet long and laden with general cargo, he at present lies right across the waterway, and notwith- standing assistance rendered by swain tugs is likely to so remain until the tide rises about one o'clock. She is drawing just over twenty feet. Captain Waring, Ship Canal superintendent, is aboard.

TETE SHIP CANAL COMPANY AND RUNCORN. ANOTHER MEETING OF THE COUNCIL. THE GRAVING DOCK QUESTION SETTLED.

A specially convened meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council was held on Tuesday evening for the purpose el again considering the graving dock question, and to receive a report of the deputation which waited upon t,e Ship Canal Company the previous day. Mr. unison presided,and there were also present : Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., S. Taylor, J.P., C.C.. J. Prit- chard, F. Wiswall. M. J. Sweetman, H. J. Grice, R. H. ' Posnett, G. H. 9 Kirkham, W. Collier, T. Dean, G. Hewitt, Dr. McKenzie, D. Bisbrown, J. Wilson, W. M. Williams, and F. A. Lake (law • clerk), G. Marshall (secretary), and M. Barker (surveyor). Mr. Lake read the report of the committee which waited upon the Canal Company, which was as follows : —" Your committee beg to report that on the invitation of the Ship Canal Company they with the Law Clerk (Mr. Lake) attended at the company's offices in Man- chester and there had an interview with Mr. Bythell (chairman), Mr. Southern, a director of the company, Sir Leader Williams, Mr. Whit- worth, Mr. Marshall Stevens, and Mr. Meadowcroft. The members of the committee present were Messrs. Imison; Speakman, Taylor, Lightburn and Pritchard. It was decided to deal with the items named in the letter from the Council of the 19ch inst. seriatim. —Mr. Bythell at once stated that the Company could not accept the condition named in item one, viz., that for a ten years' option of establishing a graving dock,west of the railway bridge, on the groued that it would interfere with the development ol the Company's docks, etc. Your committee pointed out that an absolute waiver of the graving dock could not be granted, except on some sueli condition, and suggested as an altereative the sus- pension of the graving dock clause in the agreement of 1890 for 20 or 25 years. After some discussion, Mr. Bythell agreed to advise the Company to accept the alternative proposal, and ultimately, after consultation with Mr. Southern, and the Company's officials, gave a decided acceptance on behalf of the Company of the pro- posal. All the other items mentioned in the letter or the 19th inst. were then considered, and subject to various explanations which are specified in Mr. Whitworth's letter of the 25th inst.,hereto annexed,were accepted by the Company... The proposed works wilt result in a con- tinuous deep layby, inclusive of the existing one, of 1,530 feet. Your Committee consider the terms as settled

by them highly satisfactory and advantageous to the town and trade of Runcorn, and strongly recommend the Council to allow the agreement of the Ist September, 1890,as set forth in the aforesaid terms." The Law Clerk then read the following communica- tion from Mr. Whitworth, dated March 25th. " Dear Sir,—l have the pleasure to confirm the arrangements which your deputation made with the Chairman of the Company to-day, subject to the approval of your Coun- cil. The most convenient course will be to deal with the items as mentioned in your letter of the 19th inst., and state (1) which of them have been agreed to without charge, and (2) the modifications which have now been mutually agreed upon. Item 1. The proposal that land should be Eet apart for a graving deck west of Runcorn Bridge is snail- doued, and in lieu theretif it is agreed that the construc- tion of the graving dock mentioned in the agreement of 1890 shall be postponed for a term of 25 years. Item 2. Your proposals are agreed to with the ex- ception that the Company have the option to reinstate the eld manure wharf with sufficient depth for barges, as a fulfilment of the obligation to provide a wharf. Item 3 and 4 agreed to. Item 7 agreed to. Item 8. fhe Company agree to maintain 124 feet depth and the new jetties. The coal tip is to be the one previously in use at, the Weston docks, but it is to be timed with a duplicate shoot for loading salt. Tne Company will pay the costs connected wail the proposed agreement. The plan of the pro- posed new wharfage, jett,ies, and coal tip was sub- mitted to the deputation to-day and is enclosed herewith fur further inspection and fDr the approval of your Council. It is understood that the plan for these works is to be approved before the agreement is signed, so as to avoid any argument afterwards." Mr. Speakman said he had the greatest possible pleasure in moving the adoption of this report. He was glad to say that in committee the report was practic- ally accepted unanimously, and it was for the Boatel now to repeat what they had done in committee. For the reascus he had stated in committee it would be wise her them to do so. In the first place the Ship Canal Company were conceding what a great number of the innauitants of Runcorn would consider a wise proceed- ing, thattwas an extended lay-by. The Council had secured really as much again as was first offered in the form of lay-by, and that the Council ever thought of getting. Instead of the coal tip being brought right amongst the clay and other delicate things that were , shipped and transhipped at the lay-by,it was to be fixed almost at Weston Point. The committee were also de- sirous that besides the coal tip there should be provision for the shipment of salt, and the Company had conceded a shoot by which the largest vessels that entered the Ship Canal could be load.d at Weston Point,in the same manner as coal and other goods of that kind could be shipped. He thought there was a distinct advantage. Ml the other little things that had" been spoken of had been agreed to so that there was now nothing between the Council and the Company, and he hoped the mem- bers would accept the plans and the report unaisi- mously. Mr. Pritchard said it was with a very great deal of satisfaction that he seconded the motion because he believtd that these trading taeilities would be a greater boon than a graving dock alone. The trading facilities offered to the Council now were two shipways, and 1,530 feet of lay-by with coal and salt tip provided on Abe Ship Canal, and other advantages besides. He should like the Council to come to a unanimous vote upon a matter of this imp-.r ance, arid he urged it on this grouteLthat the directors of the Ship Canal had met the Council in the most generous and liberal manner with regard to the cencessions they had made. (Hear, hear.) The directors took their stand up to the previ- eats day upon art absolute waiving of the-graving dock, but they had recognised the strong feeling that existed in the town on the subject, and the deputation induced them to agree to a supervision of the clause, (which would be in force in the future equally as it had been in the past), and this without withdrawing but really add- ing to the other facilities that were asked for previously. 'I hey had made lacings to the jetties 25 feet instead of 20 trot, and Sir Leader Williams had shown the deputa- tion that the cost would be considerably more than was contemplated a fortnight ago. He (Mr. Pritchard) con- sidered the Ship Canal directors were favourably dis- posed to Runcorn. He was sure they had the goodwill of Mr. Bythell—(hear, hear) and if anything could be shown for the good of the community of Runcorn, he was certain they would have the support of Mr. Bythell, and the weight of his influence as chairman of directors. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Lightburn supported the proposition with the greatest pleasure and remarked that the Council might congratulate themselves and the people of Runcorn on , the favourable termination of very anxious negotia- , tions. . - ..

Mr. Taylor observed that Runcurn in the future would need to feel thankful for this agreement being carried out. Mr. Lightburn, supplementing what he had prt viously sail, exinained that any, authoritative and well con- sidered grievance from members of carrying bodies or other trade, if put before the Council would receive kind consideration and tits consideration of Mr. Bethel!. Mr. Williams: Is it not a fact that we had some statistics from Mr.Ab€,l in reference to diminished traffic. I consider this one of the greatest grievances that exists. Has that been placed before the directors ? • Mr.Lightburn replied that besides Mr. Abel's statistics other facts were placed before Mr.Bythell,and as he has previously stated they pressed them with all the energy which was consistent with propriety. (fiear,hear.) Mr. Williams thought this grievance should be forced home. If they had made a good bargain let them try to make a better one.

Mr. Grice said that he had a strong feeling that the course taken by the Council would be very satisfactory to the general public of Runcorn. Mr. Collier said it was such a uovzlty to be in a majo- rity that he could not help taking advantage of the sen- sation—(laughter)—and express great pleasure in sup- porting the motion. Mr. Bisbrown thought, even at the risk of being con- sidered a mutual admiration society, the Council ought to congratulate the Committee on the work accomp- lished. They had a strong public feeling at their backs in the action they took. Mr. Posnett pointed out that one point had not been mentioned, and that was about the plans being satis- factory to the meeting before the agreement was signed. Were the plans satisfactory to the deputation who met the Ship Canal directors ? Mr. Taylor : I think that is the Committee's opinion. Mr. Speakman : We were satisfied as a Committee in Manchester yesterday, but as we were to meet to-night we obtained Cho plans so that you might be satisfied as well. Dr.Mclienzie said he was quite in accord with all that had been done by the Committee. They had obtained much m, re than was expected and there was a great deal of credit due to them.

Mr. Pritchard remarked that as to carrying out the work, Mr. Southern, chairman of the Finance Commit- tee of the Manchester Corporation attended:at the con- ference on the previous day.and there was no doubt the money would be set apart as soon as the vdte of that Council was known; and Mr. Bythell gave an assurance that the contracts would be put in hand at once, and the work go forward. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman said one of the features of the case, which had not been brought prominently before the Council, and one which augured well for the tuture,vras I this: the unwillingness of the directors to reserve a piece :vr ,f ten_ ; c4ka.

WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY.

The monthly meeting of the above Society Was held in the committee room of the Museum, on Tuesday evening. There was a good muster of mem- bers, and the chair wee occupied by the president, Mr. J. Fairhurst. The chairman in opening the pro- ceedings announced that very advantageous terms had been made with the Museum Committee, with regard to the use of the committee room for the meetings of the Society. He also thanked the Council for electing him the president for the en- suing year. He said that he would do all he poe- sibly could to promote the well-being of the society. He said that lecture work would be the ,ilbject for the evening. This was a subject which was an in- teresting one and in which many members, him- self included would like to have an increased know- ledge. The programme of the evening was then proceeded with, and consisted of slides exhibited upon the screen, illustrative of " Jane Conquest," and "Gabriel Grubb," the slides being kindly lent by Mr. Dalton, the treasurer. A few slides belonging to members were also exhibited upon the screen. Mr. 1). Stone was the lantern manipulator. At the close of the meeting Mr.Fair- burst called attention to a paragraph upon the dr- cular,Frorn Mr. Pritchard,the secretary, stating that he had been offered, and had ficcepted, the manage- ment of the Edge Hill bookstall of Messrs. W. 11. Smith . and Son, and should probably be away before the issue of the next circular. He therefore, took this opportunity of bidding each member "Forewell,"and of thanking one and all for the kindness shown to him during tne four years he had acted as secretary. He trusted that the same help would be given to whoever might suc- ceed him. That the seciety might go on and pros- per was his only wish, and though far away, he should watch the linings of the society and look back upon the time st;ent. in their midst with plea- sure. Mr. Fairhurst said that he was sorry that Mr. Pritchard was leaving them, and be proposed a vote of thanks to him for his past seavices. lie hoped that he would prosper in his new sphere. The motion was seconded and carried with accla- mation. Mr. Pritchard in reply, thanked the mem- bers for their vote, and said that he regretted leav- ing them. The meeting was then brought to a termination.

pointed to an immediate development of Runcorn when the Company were in a position to do it, and when the demands of the place would justify the expenditure. (Hoar, hear.) This showed that the deep docks were not so far in the future as some of them had begun to fear. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Speakman on being called upon to reply, said he had only. to ask that they should be unanimous, particuarly now that they had got Mr. Collier in. (Laughter.) The whole of the members voted for the motion, with the exception of Mr. Kirkham, who did not vote. Mr. Speakman then moved the following resolution, endorsing the decision which had been come to. Mr. Bisbrown seconded the proposition which was carried. Mr. Kirkham again did not vote. THE BURIAL BOARD AND THE RATES. . .

- ----- Mr. Lake explained that at the last meeting of the Council with regard to taking over the Burial Board, a misapprehension arose as to the rate on which the expense would fall. Owing to the sudden measure in which the matter was carried they had not time to look up all the authorities on the matter, and the Council was then advised that probably the expenditure would fall upon the district rate instead of the poor rate, the reason for that being that part of certain Burial Acts were incorporated in the Public Health Act, and also to the anomaly of one rate levying authority having to ask another authority to levy a rate for them to spen I. On looking into the varieus Acts of Parliament he found the rate would fall on the poor rate and not on the district rate. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman thought the Law Clerk had been some- what at a disadvantage. They were none of them as fit as they would like to have been at midnight, after a long dav's work—(laughter),—especially as they had not the books at hand which were necessary in order Ito arrive at the whole of the facts of the case at the time. Mr. Lake merely gave his opinion in a casual sort of way ; he did not commit himself, and he was sure they were all glad to hear the explanation and to learn that gone of the dire results!which were prophesied were likely to come upon them. (Laughter.) Mr. Williams : I think the mover of the resolution was aware of the fact. Mr. Wilson said he doubted the statment made at the last meeting and felt convinced there was an error some- where. He was glad the matter had been squared up so straightforwardly and the misapprehension removed. Mr. Speakman was glad the matter had been put right. He had not the slightest objection to the moving of the resolution, it was to theelateness of the hour he objected to. _ _

The meeting then went into Committee to consider the standing orders.

A CLAIM FOR SALVAGE.

A RUNCORN CREW COMPLIMENTED.

At the Liverpool County Court, on Friday, before his Honour Judge Collier, and Messrs. Reay and Macginnis, nautical assessors, the owners, master and crew of the Manchester Ship Canal Company's hopper No. 1, belonging to Runcorn, brought an action to recaver the sum of £3OO from the Uverpool Steam Tug Company, for salvage services rendered to the steam tug Pathfinder whilst she was in _ a sinking condition in the River Mersey, on February sth. The. value of the tug was stated to be £5OO as she then lay, and that of the Hopper £4,000. Mr. A. G. Steele represented the claimants,and Mr. Miller, of the firm of Messrs. Miller, and Williamson, appeared for the defendants. _ . . .

'Charles Peacock, Ben., captain of the hopper, deposed that on the day in question he observed the tug, Pathfinder, coining down the river and a flat proceeding in the opposite direction. Toe two forci- bly collided, with the result that the Pathfinder was very seriously damaged, so touch so that she was in a sinking condition. Be immediately proceeded to the spot to render what assistance he could. On reaching her he round that sir had been deserted by her crew who had left by means of their own boat. He requested his son Charles to go on board, and fasten ropes to her. This having boen accomplished, he towed , the tug to Duke's D...cks, where she was safely berthed This was done at great risk to the lives of the crew of the hopper, and more especially to that of his son, should anything have happened in the shape of the boiler bursting through the contraction caused by the cold water, as she was fast filling. Charles Peacock, jun., and Joseph Shaw; jun., of Picow-street, Runcorn, a Mersey pilot, gave corrobora- tive evidence.

Mr. Miller for the defence, argued that the collision occurred through the negligence of those in charge of the flat, and further that the Pathfinder would have been rescued by their own steamer.

The judge, after a private deliberation with the. nau- tical asorssors, found that there was great danger and commended strong;y both captain and son upon the valuable services rendered. The court could not do better than award them £65 in addition to the £lO paid into court.

RUNCORN MUSIC,IL SOCIETY'S

CONCE RT.

Those who attended the Runcorn Musical Society's concert held in the Public Hall on Monday evening,were provided with a musical treat. It was the last concert of the season by the society, and it will be remembered as one in which a high standard of excellence was at- tether!. Mr.F. H. Crossley,the capable conductor of the society,was justly pleased with the performance, and at the close he stated to an " Examiner reporter that it was "the best concert the sodety has yet given." The Runcorn Musical Society has, under Mr. Crossley's 'regime vastly improved. The membership has now reached to almost hundred, and the three concerts given during the present season have been evidence of the painstaking care he has bestowed in training the members in their work. The performance on Monday night consisted of two sacred pieces—Mendelssohn's " Hear my Prayer," and Spohr's "Last Judgment." Of course, the former selection was merely introductory, consisting purely of a solo and chorus, in which Miss Creasey was the soloist. It is only fair to state that the society was assisted by a small contingent of War- ringzon vocalists and instrumentalists. The well-known "War Marcia of the Priests," in Mendelssohti's "Athalie,' served as an overture, and in this the instrumentalists , distinguished themselves, and were deservedly ap- plauded. Miss Creasey's interpretation of the pathetic and pleading appeal in the 8010 "Hear my Prayer, 0 God,incline Thine ear" won for her at once the generous applause of the auditory. The honours in regard to this must be shared by the chorus, who succeeded in bring- ieg it to a fitting climax. Of the chief piece of the erening—Spohr's "Last Judgment " —it cannot be better described than in the words of Professor Taylor, who in his preface to the English edition 'if Sp:uir's second oratorio " Calvary says :—" Perhaps there is no instance of a similar work, under like circumstances, having attained such speedy celebrity and such high estimation, as 'The Last Judg- ment.' Before the performance of this oratorio at the Norwich Festival, in 1830, Spohr was little heard of in this country beyond the orchestra and audience of the Philharmonic Concerts ; while, as a sacred writer, he was unknown. Yet, produced without the sanction of metropolitan approbation, new to every performer and every auditor, it at once seized the public attentioreand aommanded the admiration of the most distinguished professors of every school. Its influence upon the feel- ings of an audience has been attested by expressions more decided and unequivocal than I ever remember to have witnessed. I speak not or the admiration which the aturrician deri Visor frem snob a display ef the power and resources of his art, nut to the 11..innge which nature though musically untutored, inv. lontarily,yet pays to geoius. . The throbbine heart. the moistening eye, the qui verisg lip, here bespeak the triumph if the cornp:eter." The leading vocalisls were Mies Bettie Creasey, Miss Amy Banks, Mr. E. Dunkerton, and Mr. Bell Grove`, a quartette who proved themselves equal to the exigencies of the occasion. The work contains many graceful and telling solo passages, full of delicate and deeply pathetic touches, which were made the most of try the vocalists. Of the concerted music, the fascinating quaitette •• Yes, every tear and every sorrow the Lord shall wipe away from their eyes," and the thrilling duet, " Forsake me not in this dread hour," are worthy of special mention. In this latter piece, Miss Creasey (soprano), and Mr. Dun- kerton (tenor), in response to the emphatic applause of the audience, bowed their acknowledgements. The c4ottis 'throughout acquitted itself admirably. 'rhe idbustness of attack, and the s Ade gradations of tone being all that could be wisheii for. The two conclud- ing choruses, "Great and wonderful are all Thy works " and "Thine is the kingdom, the power and the glory," were pieces which gave the chorus an opportunity of displaying their powers, end it must be admitted that they gave a very creditable rendition of them. The orchestra was well t (ripped for its important work, for the " Last Judgment " is an oratorio abounding in diffi- cult—though delightful—passages. In the tuneful over- ture, and the majestic symphony prior to the solo " It ia ended," and also in the onerous choruses, they played with musicianly taste and skilLand much of the success of the concert is attributable to them. Mr. Frank H. Crossley, the conductor, wielded the baton throughout the evening, and to him a meecl of praise is due for the able and energetic mariner with which he piloted all, through manifold difficulties,

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A WIFE'S DISSOLUTE BEHAVIOUR. At the Warrington Bor-augh Police Court. on Friday,last week before Alderman Flarrison (chairman) andother nine' istrates, Harriet Hill 36 Watkin-st., was charged with having on the 14th March and divers dates neglected her children, Elizabeth, Mary, Joseph, John, and Thomas in a manuer likely to injure their health.—Mr. Buckton, solicitor, prosecuted on behalf of the N.3.P.C.C., and said the defendant's husband was a labourer and a decent man. The neglect complained of was doe to the constant habits of drunkenness by the defendant. The husband worked regularly and gets 235. a week, and his wife spent it all in drink and pawned anything that he bought to supply the house with. Sometimes she had gone out of the house in the morning and not re- turned until midnight. On one occasion a travelling packman called at the house and defendant bought goods from him to the extent of 17s. 61. which she had to pay in weekly instalments. The next day she pawned 1 them all. The husband natura ly wished to do the best he could but he could not manage his wife. The chit- -siren were very dirty and the house was left in charge of the second girl aged 11 while the eldest aged 15 worked as a fustian cutter.—lnspector McAteer deposed that on the 14th of the present month he went in company with Detective-Sergeant Monaghan to defendant's house and there found three children, Mary 11, John 4, and Joseph 7, all in a deplorable condition, and the girl that was in charge of the house had nothing on but a frock. She seemed starved and there was neither fire nor wood in the house. Witness had visited the house about 12 times in the last six months and many times he had remonstrated with the woman to no effect. Detective Sergeant Monagan corroborated. The husband of the defendant was called and he stated that he could do no good at all with his wile, and it was no use his trying, and the statements made by the Inspec- tor were true. If he bought anything she pawned it and during the recent cold weather she had taken the child's shoes off and pawned them.—Alderman Harrison in expressing his condemnation of the defendant's con- duct said she deserved to be turned out of the house. She being willing to go to a home the case was ad- journed for a month in order that Mr. Taylor, the P.C.M. might take her to the C.E.T.S. shelter. MISPLACED CHARITY. At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Saturday before Mr. J. Burgess and other magistrates. Henry Charles Withers was charged with stealing 225. from Rebecca Locker, 8, Mill-street.on the 12th of March inst. Defendant pleaded guilty.—Mr. Hunt stated that the complainant was a green-grocer and occupied a shop in Mill-street. The defendant was a hawker, 18 years of age and living at School Brow. On the Ist of the pre. sent month he applied to Mrs. Locker for work and she seeing that he looked a decent lad took him in and gave him food and board. On the 12th inst defendant went out with Mrs. Locker's donkey and cart loaded with goods to the value 225. Nothing was heard of him until about 5 A'clock the same day when complainant received information that her donkey and cart were under the archway at Bank Quay station. The complainant then went and found the animal but no trace of the defendant or the goods. Detective Monaghan was informed and he subsequently apprehended the defendamt who when Charged said" I sold the goods for a sovereign and left the donkey and cart near Bank Quay Station and went to Manchester, got some beer, and was robbed of half a sovereign."—The Bench:sent him to gaol,for 14 days'hard labour. AN ALLEGED FRAUDULENT FORGEMAN. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Monday morning, before the Mayor and a full bench of magis- trates, Thomas Stephens, no fixed abode, was charged with stealing by means of a trick, from Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Bewsey Forge, on Thursday afternoon, the sum of 4s. in cash.—The Chief. Constable stated to the magistrates that defendant had been employed by the Company to take bye-turns and on the Thursday after- noon the relief cashier was paying some of the men and among them the defendant, who, Mr. Hunt stated, had called out the number of another man and received his money 4s. A man named George Williams then said "You are not 24, you are 44," and defendant said" You can't count." Mr. Wallington, the relief cashier, then asked defendant if he worked for Donoghue (24), and defendant said " Yea " and walked away. Later, Donoghue (24) came for his money and found that de- fendant had received it. Information was given to Detective Monaghan and Sergeant Lawrence from the forge, and defendant was caught on Sunday night„—Mr. T. Wallington said that in the absence of the regular cashier he had paid the money that day. In his evidence he corroborated Mr. Hunt, adding however that defend- ant was entitled to 4s. but not as 24.—Stephens denied that the money be received was not his own, and the magistrates considering there was a:doubt in the case dismissed the defendant.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 30 1895 RUNCORN PETTY SESSIONS. NEW BOOKS) HAGAZINES) ETC. MONDAT.—Before Mr. John' Brundrit (chairman), and' Mr. F. J. Nerman. CASSELL AND CO.—Duriug the last month or two the prevailing trend of the publishers' and book-makers' art has been decidedly towards making the standard shape of a short novel just about twice as long as broad. The result is a novelty—a pleasing contrast—which is as welcome as most other departures from the hard and fast lines of custom whose law has hitherto been as binding as that of the Merles and Persians. Mesers. I Cassell and Co., always along with the times, have fallee in with the popular taste, and in " A King's Diary," by Mr. Percy White, they inaugurate a series of booklets which promise to be highly popular, and which will go to form a Pocket Library. finder the able editorship of Mr. Max Pemberton, this edition with its attractive outside appearance, will be a success we venture to predict, as pronounced as that of any other venture among the myriads which these publishers have engaged in. In the first number the editor announces that the foundation stone of this little library is to be simple stories, deriving force from their directness and from that personal narrative in which they will be em- bodied. Now " A King's Diary" comes under this cate- gory, and though simple in one way, its simplicity is not sufficient to let the reader see through the narrative in an instant—rather the opposite—for it is not until the end of the story is reached that any conception of the apt- ness of the title is gathered. The substance of the book is founded on the fickle footing of every-day soeiel life, and to weave from this such an amusingly taking novel, at once stamps the author as a man of genius and erudition. In it the writer expresses some very odd thoughts arid definitions, while his connecting the " hero" with a journalistic enterprise of doubtful status at the commencement and complete downfall in the end. providesa n opportunity for describing the modus verandi of that venture, which lie 'leak's; every adve etage of, and without which the key-note oi the whole story would be lost. Without going into further particulars we can confidently say, that for an afternoon's quiet en- -1 joyment by a person who understands and appreciates i amusing delineation of character, a more suitable sub- ject could not be obtaieed. (The price is is. 41)—Year by year we have received from Messrs. Cassell and Co., the souvenir of the last play produced at the Lyceum Theatre by Mr. Henry Irving. This year brings us the souvenir of " King Arthur" (J. Cormens Carr), about which so much has been said and which was first pre- vented at the leveeum Theatre, on the 12th January of the present year. Like its predecessors, " King Arthur" is bound in a cover, bright with colouring of a taste- ful and terrifying description. The artists who have lent their skill to the adornment of its pages, are Messrs. J. Bernard Partridge and Hawes Craven, gentlemen who in the past, have contributed much towards the success of the bye-gone souvenirs, and to whom great praise is due for the fine and striking illustrations of the chief characters and scenes in the play. Very weird are two of these by Hawes Craven,—" The Magic Mere," with the gleaming hand rued sword rising out of its dark,o'er- casteand still waters, :and "The Passing of Arthur," which took place on board the barge gliding slowly out on the ocean to the touch of the three queens. I synop- sis of the scenery is given, with explanatory verse at the commencement,and adds not a little to the excellence of the whole production.—" Battles of the Nineteenth Cen- tury " makes a most interesting work, whose first num- ber is issued this month. Its most notable contributors are famous war-correspondents than whom no one is better fitted to describe battles, which apart from being significant in history, abound in incidents of personal valour and daring. The battles to be described are fought in all parts of the workaunder varied conditions, and during the stirring epoch of the nineteenth century. Amongst those whose services have already beereengaged may be mentioned ;—Archibald Forbes, G. A. Plenty, E. F. Knight, Major Arthur Griffiths, D. H. Parry. , Charles Lowe, C. J. Cutcliffe-Hyne, Herbert Russell, , I Angus Evan Abbott, Herbert Compton, Max Pembertc n,l Stoddard Dewey, A. Hilliard Atteridge. ' and C. Stein. To the illustrator is half the success of a hook due and we can say with all truthfulness that in this direction also have the pub- lishers left nothing undone to secure artists who from actual 'experience have particular ability in depicting strife's fearful carnage, with the vivid colouring of reality. Accurate and life-like portraits of generals are to be scattered through the series with a liberal sprink- ling of maps and plans which add much to the compre- hension of the writer's description. Part I. has as a frontispiece the stirring action of Sergeant Ewart can- tering the Imperial Eagle at Waterloo ; the deed is pre- sented in glowing colours, which, with the subject of illustration, make a magnificent plate with which to commence a record of battles. Maier Arthur Griffiths supplies the introduction, while the text is graphically written and the letterpress clear and readable.—(7d. montlely).—A cheap edition of" Robinson Crusoe " has been commenced, adding still another to the long list of illustrated monthlies, though this is above the average in excellence and below it in price. Colin Campbell (Lord Clyde) by Archibald Forbes, who has great scope afforded him in this book for displaying his descriptive powers and knowledge of technical de- tail. This work has additional value RS a most com- plete historical record of all the campaigns, &c., taken pert in by Lord Clyde, who commenced his eventful career as ensign in the 9th foot or Norfolk regiment,and terminated it as field-mahhal and peer, on the 14th August, 1863. He was rightly termed the foremost soldier in England and the work of his life is faithfully traced out by the author, who also mentions numerous incidents which have not before been published. "Colin Campbell" is one of a seriesof similar biographies known as" English men of Action," and on opening the book is seen a speaking reproduction of the hero of the memoirs, after a picture by Sir Francis Grant, P.R.A. The military contents have their commencement in the Peninsula, and wind up at Simla, and from thence to that permanent abode of:England a great,—Westminster Abbey.
