Renwick, MA;, L.L.8., has been requisitioned. It is announced in the preface that a special feature of this iieue is the information appended to the commercial statistics of various countries as to their systems of valuation,and this infoetnatien is the result of an enquiry on the subject which was instituted during the vear,and which is indeed a valuable addition to a book in which further particulars of any nature are indispensable. In the navies of the world which have been revised by Mr. John Leyland, a system of classification of warships has neen adoptecl rendering easier a comparison between the navies of different powers. All manner of informa- tion is contained within the volumes which forms the most reliable and comprehensive compilation of facts and figures relative to the commerce, industry, . popula- tion, religion, finance, crime, military and naval defen- sive and offensive arrangements, of practically every government in existence. When it is considered thae there are over 1,000 pages of closely printed, though legible type in the edition, the editor has reasot. to thank the army of widespread collaborators who have lent their aid to its production. Nothing appears to be want- ing and a Statesman—or for the matter of that any other gentleman—who takes an interest in the affairs of his country,is suffering an inestimable loss If he is not poss- essed of this "Year book," and to otherwise obtain the information contained in which,iwould involve a great multiplication of its initial cost, as heretofore, 103. 61 GEORGE NEWNES AND Co.—" The Art Bible," by these publishers is rightly named, for it is c.rtainly a maga- zine whose predominant feature is art in every sense of the word. GOOD TEMPLARY AT LATCHFORD. -- PRESENTATION TO MR. L. ELLISON. On Monday evening the members of the Bonehili Branch Temperance Lodge, Latchford, assembled in the Temperance Hall, linutstord-road, when a public meet- ing was held, on the occasion of a presentation to Bro. Luke Edison. There was a good attendance of old and young, and the chair was taken by Mr. Richardson. After devotional exercises the programme of the even- ing was commenced by the usual chairman's address, after which a song was given by Miss Alice Jones who suitably initiated a very happy evening. The re- mainder of the programme was composed as follows : Songs by Miss L. Hough.and Bro. Ditchfield ; recitations by Miss Mary Jane Clark and Miss Maggie Tickle. The most interesting event of the evening was, of course, the presentation to Bro. Luke Ellison who pos- sesses the respect and esteem of a very large circle of acquaintances. In the neighbourhood he is known as a man who, as a professed teetotaler, has adhered to his principles with steadfast and unswerving faith. The presentation was made by the Chairman who handed to Bro. Ellison a handsome gold shield, bearing on one side the inscription " Preented to Bro. Ellison for 21 years' membership completed, Nov. 1894," and on the other an appropriate device. In making the presentation Mr. Richardson, after expressing the pleasure it gave him, said he made the gift on behalf of the Good Templars of Latchford on Mr. Ellison completing 21 years' good ser- vice in that lodge. He thought it was but a poor way of Axpressing what they felt—they could not give Mr. Ellison what he deserved. Bro. Edison had laboured earnestly in the temperance cause for that length of time, and it was as little as they could do to acknow- ledge it in whatever way they could. The speaker made the presentation in the hope that Mr. Ellison might live long and do good as he had done in the past. He was a blessing to the Lodge and to many in the neighbour- hood. (Applause.) Temperance addresses were given with great earnest- ness by Bros. Pearson, Corbett, A. Ford, H. Meese and Sandford, who referred in the warmest terms to Bro. Ellison's services. In responding. Mr. Ellison thanked the Chairman for making the presentation and taking the chair at the meeting, and also expressed his thanks to the committee who had taken part in collecting the money, for the purpose of making the presentation,as he was sure it had been a very arduous duty which had been done nobly. He also thanked the speakers who had spoken so highly of him. He could, he said, hardly express himself in words but sill he felt very thankful for what had been done. The obligations weighed very heavily upon him because they showed that those who had been working with him had appreciated the little services he Pad been able to render. His connection with L ttchford dated back as far as 1838. He was not a Latchford born man but had been there since he was two years of age and it was very different then. There were no achools at that time and no education to be , obtained, but lie was pleased to know that boys and girls to-day had better advantages aim he had when a boy. When nine years old he was put to work,and at 11 apprenticed to a shoe-maker, and at that time he corn- menc.ed his temperance career though he was a life-long teetotaler and had never taken any drink. 'flie first temperance movement at Latchford was the gathering held regularly at the barn which a short time ago stood in the place of the new chapel. Their commencement there wee in 1874 and the present temperance hall was built in 1879 after a large amount of opposition, which was the initial cause of its erection, fe-day he felt more inclined to this work than ever and he was more proud of temperance work now than at any time previously. A vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman on the motion of Mr. Edison, seconded by Mr. Few.—The meeting closed with the Singing of the Doxology.

Among the illustrated articles, the one of predominating interest to Warringtcmians is that relating to Luke Fildes, R.A., the famous artist who owes his preliminary educa- tion to the " good old town." The sketch is pleasingly descriptive of Mr. Fildes works, from magazine illustrating to " The Doctor" which is regarded by many as his greatest work, and the portraiture of the Duke and Duchess of York,and "The Princess of Wales," which was exhibited in last year's Academy. A truth- ful portrait of the artist is given. T. FISIDIR UNWIN AND Co.—The " Century " for March has two particularly fresh and unhackneyed sub- jects. In these globe-trotting days tke account of " a new field of travel " will be greeted with pleasure by American travellers, and this, Miss Harriet Waters Preston and Mr. Joseph Pennell, the artist, have found " Beyond the Adriatic," along the cost of Dalmatia and Albania,a region, moreover, which is now accessible to travellers. The second topic is the art of the late Jean Carries, the French sculptor and potter. The " Cen- tury " is the first of the magazines, we believe, to give attention to the important work of this artist, the ap- pearance of whose work in the Salon of the Champ de Mars of 1892 caused the fellow artists to demand and obtain for him from the President of the Republic the cross of the Legion of Honor. The text of the article is written by Emile Hovels que, a French art critic,who, by the way, writes in English, considering the character and influence of Carrel work upon the art of his time. Nine pictures of the sculptor's work—two from ex- amples owned in America—are presented with Vie article. GEORG& G. Mrs. Wright, of Southport, and Mrs. Houghton, of Broom Cottage, have forvererded parcels of welcome and useful papers. THE CHURCH HISTORY LECTURE.—•Mr H. J. West- brook delivered on Msnday evening in the Institute,his eighth lecture of the series on Cbutch History, dealing with" The Commonwealtn, the Restoration, and the Revolution," and covering the period A.D. 1649-1691. Before proceeding with the lecture, he said that antecedent intolerance of Puritans was not a valid excuse for want of toleration lin Churchmen afterwards, yet a clear statement ,of the excesses on both sides was of value in assisting towards a mutual agreement to avoid alleging past cruelty as a reason for an unfriendly attitude towards each other now. The lecture detailed the doings of the Long Parliament,or rather the " Independent " remnant thereof, and extracts were read from the" Diary of John Evelyn" to show how Churchmen were persecuted and harassed. A:noneat ether things the clergv were forbid- den.toperform the Church Service even in pelvate houses; and the use 01 the Prayer-book in private, as well as in public, was prohibited. Holy baptism was forbidden, and also the celebration of the Holy Communion, as well as the solemnization of holy matrimony. The one cit. termination of the party in power was to prevent the Church reviving. Next came the end of the Protector- ate, the Restoration of the King, the Savoy Conference. Revision of the Prayer Book,the Scourges of London,Sir Christopher Wren's Architecture, the National Dread of Romanisin, the Rye House Plot, tLie Persecution or the Puritans, the Persecution of Churchmen, the Dt-ctaration of Indulgence, the Bishop's Petition, the Bishops in the Tower, their trial and acquittal, the Prince of Orange, Flight of James H., the Toleration Act, the Nonjurors, the Jacobites,and Bishop Ken, from whose will was read the following clause testifping to his faith and church- manship As for my religion. I die in the Holy Catholic and Apostolic Faith; professed hy the whole Church before the disunion of East and West : more particularly / die in the Cotomunion of the Church of England as it stands distinct from all Papal and Puritan innovations, and as it adhers to the doctrine of the Cross."

A RECENT RUNCSRN NEGLECT OF CHILDREN CASE. PETITION TO THE SECRETARY OF STATE.

In the case of Ellen Stringer, who was convicted before Messrs. Brundrit and Thomas Handley. on the 4th March, for neglecting her children, under a summons taken out by the Royal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, and sentenced to a ' month's im- prisonment, a petition was forwarded to the Secretary of State asking for the decision to be repealed. The docu- ment stated that the family were poor and unable to secure legal assistance,the result being in their opinion a miscarriage of justice. In support of their prayer the petitioners set torth that the children had all attended school regularly, and associated with other scholars, which would not have been permitted if they had been in the condition described by the prosecution. The chil- dren since their parents' conviction had been examined by Dr. McKenzie, who did not find any trace of vermin. He certified that they were well nourished and in a healthy condition. The people had no money to spen in drink; the woman was of sober habits, and seemed fond of her children. Among the signatories to the petition were Dr. McKenzie, Mr. Walter Collier, Mr. James Percival, and Mr. G. W. Allen. The Home Office sent down the petition to Mr. Ashton, the clerk to the magistrates, for his report upon the case, which was forwarded in due course. As a result he has received a communication signed by Kenelm E. Digby, dated March 20th, in which he says, "I am directed by the Secretary of State to acquaint you, for the information of the Justices, that under all the circumstances he has de- clined to advise any interference with the sentence of imprisonment passed by them on the woman."

HALTON PARISH COUNCIL.

On Tuesday evening a meeting of this Council was held in the Girls' Schoolroom, Halton. The Rev. G. D. Wray occupied the chair, and there were also present:— Messrs. Joseph Astbury, W. McCullock, J. S. Jeacock,R. Hughes, J. Barrow, J. Acimrley, J. Looker and Mr. Woodier (cleric) —The accounts submitted for payment included one from Mr. Ashton, clerk te the Runcorn Guardians, for the sum of £l5 Us. Bd., expenses in- curred at the Parish Council election. Objection was raised to several items in t he account, and it was decided on the motion of Mr. Hughes, that the Chairman and Clerk' see Mr. Ashton on the inatter.—ln reply to a letter sent by the Clerk having reference fo the repair of a footpath leading from Halton-road to Halton, Mr. C. L. Evans, Norton estate office, weote stating that he did not promise to ao what was necessary to the footpath unless he was wrong in his contention, and he must still decline to do anything in the matter unless the Council could prove to him that the tenant or landowner was liable. He had no wish whatever to oppose the Council unnecessarily but the question of footpaths was a serious matter on a large property.--Mr. John Lea, the Terrace, Halton, wrote respecting the repair of a fence leading up to the girls' school, and said he was informed that the Churchwardens put up the fence that was blown down. The Parish Council would do the right thing by asking them to put it up.—Mr. J. Gard- ner D. Eogleheart writing from the Duchy of LaLcaster Office, London, asked the clerk whether he would be good enough to forward a sketch or plan of the locus in quo of Hanging Rock-road, as without it, he was at a loss to know to what road reference was intended to be made. It was to be presumed that the matter in ques- tion WAS one which had been expressly delegated to the Parish Council by the District Council, it would seem otherwise not to be a matter with which their council was competent to deal.—Mr. Hughes stated that he had seen Dr. Carruthers with reference to repairing half the upper portion of the footpath to Halton, but he declined to do arything in the matter, stating that he had noth- ing to do with the footpath.—The Chairman said if it was a footpath the Parish Council had nothing to do with it , but if it was a right of way only, the Council could acquire it by agreement.—Mr. Barrow said it would be an injustice for the Council to repair it,while some tenants were re- pairing their own.—Mr. Jeaceck proposed that they draw up a schedule of the roads wanting repair in the district, and bring such before the notice of the District Council.—Mr. McCollock seconded, and said it looked as if the Council had got out of its depth in dealing with the matter of roads. The motion after a short discus- sion was carried.—A letter from the Charity Commis- ' sion signed by Mr. J. Holford, was read. He submitted information relating to charities at Halton,and suggested ' that in view of the great number of. like letters which the Commissioners were receiving froxi other parish councils and the impossibility of giving detailed infor- mation, except after considerable delay, the Coencil should in the first place endeavour to ascertain locally full particulars of the charities in the parish,and should then consider in which, if any, of such charities they desired to intervene. If any difficulty, however, WAS experienced in procuring the information, the Commissioners would be glad to help the Parish Council in respect of such of the charities as the Council might specify. With regard to the powers of a Parish Council to appoint trustees of the charities he stated that the Commissioners before determining in the case of any charity as to the extent of such powers would think it right to ask for the observations of the trustees. If the Parish Council desired to move in the matter of any charity in the parish, and desired the advice or approval of the Commissioners before doing so, they should in the first place acquaint the Commissioners with the namer of the charities in question and state under what section or sections of the Act the Council proposed to take action.—The Chairman then read the supplementary digest respecting the Halton charities which had been sent. With respect to the library charity he said he was perfectly willing to go into that at the next, meeting of he Council. They could then decide. as to whetir they thought it was an ecclesiastical charity or not, and I if they could not Come to anything like a unanimous agreement they could appeal to the Charity Commission for their opinion. The statement respecting the chari- ties, received from the Commissioners, was really not as complete as the one he (the chairman) had circulafed.— Mr. Hughes asked whether it was possible to see a list of the names of those who received the St. Thomas' charity. The Chairman said he would provide one for the next meeting, but he thought it would be unwise to display the names of the tecipients publicly. Several members concurred in this view.—Mr. Barrow said the library charity was a warm question and he should like to see it settled.—Mr. JeAvock gave notice that he would move that some provision be made to provide the young men of the pirish with a cricket ground. He thought it would he a small expense. They had had to rely previously on the generosity of a farmer, for the loan of a field, and he thought the Council could come to some arrangement with a farmer to let them have the loan of a: field at a cheap rate.—Mr. Hughes thought young men who wanted a field to play in were as well able to pay for the ground as the ratepavers.—Mr. Hughes gave notice that at the next meeting he would nominate a person for the position 3f chairman.—This was all the business of public interest.

WARRINGTON.

P.S.A. AT YHE Y.M.C.A. Notwithstanding the unusually inclement weather en Sunday there was a very good meeting at the above—both in the mat ter of attendance and programme. The chairman was Mr. Edward Clay- ton, of Hadfield, in Derbyshire, who based his in- troductory remarks on "Socialism versns Christian Socialism,' of course advocating the latter. Mr. Richard Kent, of Levenshulme, gave an admirable address on "The best Investment,"his idea of which was the surrender of ones-self to the claims of Chaistianity, and the speaker also drew apart com- parisons from investments of a commercial nature and thoseof religion. It was unanimously accorded that the address was one of the best given in con- nection with the P.S.A. meetings. Miss Jeans, who was in good voice sang " Over the line " and " The better land," both of which met with the amrecia- tion ofthe audience. ENTERTAINMENT IN THE CO-OPERATIVE HALL. The girls and infants of St. Alban's King-street School gave a very successful entertainment in the above room on Monday evening. There was a large attendance. In the first part songs, dances, drills, &c., were given by different children in a manner which spoke well for the way in which they had been trained. The second part of the programme consisted of a piece entitled "My Aunt's Heiress," by the following children :—Misses E. Turner, M. Kelsall, A. Stirrup, E. Tierney, S. Costilloe, M. Cos- tube; M. Riley, E. Marsh M. Marsh, K. Laff, and M. Rafferty. This pleased the audience, who were not sparing in their applause. The ptertainment , was under the management of Miss Jones and the , teachers of King-st. Schools, while the dance drills and the musical dri:ls were under the skilled direc- tion of Miss Jones and Mrs. Colledge respectively.

THE SATURDAY NIGHT POPUL-AR CONCERT

On Saturday the sixteenth concert of the season was given in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-st., the room being crowded. Mr. Charles Parker, of Pen- ke,th, occupied the chair, and the good programme commenced with a cornet solo by Mr. George Cole, and the following persons also contributed:—Miss Ditchfield sang "College Bell" and "Make I believe"; Mr. Isaac Wilkinson, songs " I did it" and "For the sake of my dear little girl"; Mr. George Whitfield, humorous Lancashire relitation, " Bou-

ton's Yard"; Mr. William Whittaker gave two ex- hibitions of his skill on the concertina, for which he was twice recalled. Mr. M. Butterfield contri- buted two "recitals," and Miss Florrie Toll and Miss Sutton were the accompanists. Mr. S. B. Stringer announced that the next concert would be the last of the season, and that all the proceeds of the season's work, after meeting their liabilities, would be given to the Infirmary, seeing that the committee of that noble institution bound them- selves burdened with a rather heavy debt. They were anxious to assist them all they could, and to that end they. had decided to make the last concert a benefit concert, when they would double their charges. EVANGELICAL FREE CHURCHES. Representatives from Warrington attended the opening of the National Congress of Evangelical Free Churches, in Carr's-lane Chapel, Birmingham, on Tuesday,and these included Miss Bessie Dutton. The Rev. Dr. Agar Beet, formerly of Warrington, read a paper on 'Scholarship and Faith" to a pub- ic meeting. SEWING MACHINE!! Hand £2 18s. 6d. Treadle £4 4s.with handsome walnut cover.—J. and W. DUTTON, 16 and 18 Sankey-st.. Warrington. —Advt. MD3B GREEN'S ANNUAL BALL. The sixth annual ball in connection with Miss Green's dancing class, took place in the Conserva- tive Club Assembly Room on Tuesday evening,when from 50 to 60 couples were present. The music for dancing, which was kept up with spirit from eight o'clock to about half-past two the following morn- ing, was supplied by Messrs. Barton and Clarke's band, who were also responsible for the decorations of the room. Mr. J. Finch proved himselt an effi- cient M C., and the catering of Mr. Watkins, the, manager of the club gave universal satisfaction. THE BAPTIST MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY. This society concluded its session on Friday even- ing, when Mr. William Hickman gave a paper on " Robert Moffat, the apostle of Africa." There was a fair attendance, and the chair was taken by Mr.J. A. Heywood. The essayist gave an excellent bio- graphical sketch of the veteran missionary,rernark- inn that his example was one that all missionaries cool I follow with advantage. Moffat gave his life to the work, and not until he arrived at the scene of his life's labours did he ask a question as to his remuneration. Even when he learned he was to ' receive only £lB a year he was undaunted,and man- fully grappled with difficulties unknown to the present generation of his successors. The essayist trusted that Moffat's success, which owed nothing -to learning, wealth, influence, or position, would stimulate them all to the best of their abilities. On the proposition of Mr. H. Walker, seconded by Mr. John S Green,a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr.Hickman,and a like compliment having been paid to the Chairman, the session terminated. 'SEWING MACHINES, Hand £2 18s. 6. Treadle £4 48. wit!' handsome walnut cover.--J. and W. DUTTON, 16 and 13 Sankey-. Warrington.—A dvt. TEMPERANCE SERVICES.

The annual -temperance services were preached in the Workingmen's Gospel Temperance liall,Butter- market-street, on Sunday. The Rev. J. J. Langham, M.A., vicar of Padgate, preached in the afternoon, his text being "Ye are the salt of the earth." Temperance work, he observed, was a great work, which taxed the strength of the strongest of man- kind. Let them so speak and work as to glorify their Father, not themselves. Let their light so shine that He might he glorified, not mankind. Mr. Charles Firth, of Manchester, preached in the even- ing from the words "The days of our years are three-score years and ten." He pointed out that the present average length of human life was only 28 years. He had seen some figures showing that the rich lived to an average acre of 43, the middle class 33, while' the working classes only attained an average of 274 years. lie pointed out that teeto- talers, lived much longer than tipplers. WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF COMMERCE.

On Monday the monthly meeting of the Warring- ton Chamber of Commerce was held in the Market Gate Chambers. The deputation appointed to meet the Rivers' Committee of the Town Council in refer- ence to the negotiations with the Manchester Ship Canal Company submitted their report to the Council, who agreed to support the Corporation in any steps which they might deem necessary to protect the town. A series of questions in regard to the proposed amendment of the Canal Company's Acts, with the object of preventing the issue of fraudulent prospectuses and of protecting the interest of the public generally under the Acts,were considered. A number of suggestions were then made by the Council, and were ordered to be trans- mitted to the Associated Chambers of Commerce, which are now engaged in giving special attention to this subject. SEWING ittAmENEe. Han i£2 18s. 6d. Treadle £4 4s. with handsom w.lnut cover —J and W. DUTTON, 16 and 18, Sankev—it. Warrington Advt THE REV. M. STEVENSON ON "TEE SUNDAY' SCHOOL OF TH Furnun," A conference of clergy and Sunday school teachers, preceded by a meeting of the Council of the Diocesan Sunday SchJol Institute and tea, was held on Saturday afternoon in the Parish Church Schools, St. Helens. The chair was taken by the Right Rev. Bishop Royston, chairman and vice- president of the Diocesan Sunday School Institute, and there were present a large number of clergy- men and gentlemen, with Mr. F. Gregory Jones (hon. secretary of the Institute). After a few ' appropriate opening remarks the Chairman intro- duced the Rev. Morley Stevenson, principal of the Warrington Training College, who read a paper on " The Sunday School of the Future." After sketch- ing the original Sunday school of the past,in which the instruction was mainly secular, he contrasted it with the Sunday school of the present, which had no need to provide secular teiching, because that was provided by the State. ThrSunday school of the present was divided into two classes—the social school and the teaching school. He devoted his remarks entirely to the latter, and while dwell- ing on its iniportance,eriticized its manifest defects in organization and teaching which, in the Sunday school of the future, ought to be remedied. The teaching should be tested by examination, the school arrangements and discipline inspected while stronger efforts should be made to bind the older scholars together and keep them attached to the Church by means of guilds. Mr. T. P.A.Agnew, who followed, devoted his remarks to the necessity for more suitable buildings and better financial support, touching upon the superior arrangements for these matters in America.

MR. ASQUITH.

THE TURNING POINT IN HIS CAREER.

- In the story of Mr. Asquith's career, as it is written by Mr. Stead, some things are told not generally known, and some things, perhaps, which the person chiefly con- cerned would have desired to keep from the public, as, for example, the account of "the winning of the bride." Mr. Asquith's advance in his profession dates from the time when he became junior to Sir Charles Russell. " He did his work in such a way as to win Sir Charles's esteem, and, what was not less important, to convince Sir George Lewis that he was a coming man." "He made his mark by what appears to be the merest acci- dent. The "Times" had put forward Mr. Soames as their first witness. Sir Charles Russell had cross-exam- ined him at some length, and to little purpose. The Court adjourned for lunch, anal when Russell and his junior were taking a hasty meal, Sir Charles staggered Mr. Asquith by calmly informing him that he had better cross-examine the next witness. Now, the next witness was no other than Mr Macdonald, who, after Mr. Asquith's cross-exarnination. became famous as Simple Simon,' of the " Times.', • But this is most absurd,' said Mr. Asquith to his leader; ' he is one of the most important witnesses in the case, and, of course, you will cross-examine him yourself.' 'No,' said Sir Charles Ruasell, ' lam tired, and you will do it well enough.' Despite all Mr. Asquith's protesta- tions, •iir Charles insisted that it was to be so, and so it was accordingly. But no one knew how dismayed the junior was at finding suddenly thrust upon him a task for which he was so utterly unprepared. No one of their side had the least idea of what Mr. Macdonald would say. When Mr. Asquith rose to put his first question to the manager of the "Times," he was almost at his wit's end. By some good luck be put a question to Mr. Macdonald at the commencement of the examination which that gentleman answered in a supremely silly fashion. The answer was a revelation te Mr. Asquith, and he at once saw that he could play his fish with good results. Be did so, and all the world knows with what result."

THE EVOLUTION OF THE CYCLE.

We take the following extracts from an admirably illustrated article on cycling in last week's " Manches- ter Weekly Times." The first trace of the Cycle, we are told, is to be found, singularly enough, in the stained glass window of a church, dating back to 1642. This church is none other than the edifice at Stoke Poges, near Windsor, in whose yard the poet Grey conceived the thoughts which found matchless expression in his immortal elegy. The window represents a rough-looking wooden bench on wheels. Sitting astride it is a nude figure, propelling the bench by working his feet along the ground. A. hundred years later an Englishman de- vised a machine which was worked by two lever plank. The alternate depression of the planks caused the wheels of the cumbrous vehicle to revolve, and the by no means light task of thepropulsion was performed by a footman , the owner sitting in front and attending to the steering. A somewhat similar arrangement may be seen at work even now, in the case of invalid carriages, though, as a rule, in these latter days, the locomotion is secured by working levers with the hand. In 1818 came the celeri- pere, a French invention. This consisted of two wheels, similar to those of a modern bicycle, connected by a beam. The rider sat on the beam, and propelled him- self at a fairly good speed, by striking his feet on the ground. rhe new invention quickly found its way into England, and, rechristened the Pedestrian curricle, soon gained the favour of the gilded youth of the metropolis. The first attempt to lift the feet from " terra firina,"and to secure motion by means of levers and treadles, has been traced to the year 1819. It met with little taveur, and for a quarter of a century the idea slept the soundest of sleep. In 1845, an enterprising Scotsman, Dalzell by name, added further improvements, con- structing a, machine which bears some resemblance to to the modern safety. Report says that he attained a speed of 12 miles an hour, and won a ten mile race in a competition with the stage-coach. To a French workman, Pierre Lallement, is awarded the honour of constructing the first regular bicycle with cranks. Lallement emigrated to the United States, where he took out his patent in 1868. Some writers say that an Englishman, Edward Gilman, secured a patent for a two-wheeled crank-driven velocipede, in our coun- try, two years before the Frenchman, but there is some doubt on the point. The bicycle may be said to have first established its home in this country in the year 1869. In those days it was of clumsy construction, and was given the expressive name of bone-shaker. As a rule the wheels were of hickory, and the rims of iron The wheels were of equal dimensions. Comfort was impossible with "mounts " of this description, but no considerable improvement was effected for along period. It is not surprising to learn, therefore, that the sport did not become immediately popular. But, if not made quickly, improvements were made steadily. Steel began to take the place of wood in the construction of the wheels. The front wheel grew larger and larger.and the kind wheel smaller and smaller. Owing to these trans- formations the machine got the name of the spider, and it was the spider undoubtedly which first estab- lished the popularity of cycling. When solid rubber tyres were attached to the wheels, a great impetus was given to the pastime, and the introduction of steel ball bearings, which simplify the driving and minimise the friction, was another great step on the road to popu- larity. Then came a great change. The rear-driving safety was placed in the market, and the day of the spider very quickly departed. The new invention, or rather the new application of an old idea, became popu- lar, though the first safeties were by no means elegant . structures. Quite the reverse. But obviously they were safer than the spider. This being the case, they tempted middle-aged and even obese men to mount them, and ladies ne longer looked askance at the pastime. The next stage in tho history of the bicycle is the most important. We now come to the introduction of the pneumatic tyre, which has revolutionised the entire trade, and made bicycling the most popular pastimes. At first the inflated hollow tyre met with little favour. Even experienced riders laughed at it. For practical purposes it would never do, they said, as there was no guarantee against punctures, and people would not run the risk of having their rides suddenly ended by their machines being rendered useless in consequence of con- tact with a piece of flint, a sharp twig, or some other small obstruction common to all thoroughfares. Those Who took up this attitade had some excuse. The first pneumatic tyres were not very trustworthy. They easily pe.nctured,and their repair on a roadside was a long and tedious process. Notwithstanding th. is drawback, the inflated tyre quickly established itself in favour. It pos- sessed many advantages over every competitor. Riding it on rough surfaces was a new and delightful sensation. The jolting which was inevitable 01 uneven roads with the solid tyre was no longer noticeable, and the speed increased. For could be attained was appreciably For a time a cushioned tyre, that is to say,a thick hollow tube of indiarubber, uninflated, was tried as a substitute, but it was speedily beaten out of the field. The pneumatic became the only possible tyre, and the demand for it speedily led to great improvsments. the experiments of men who devoted themselves to the subject a tyre was produced far less liable to punctures and contrivances for rapid repair of punctures were soon invented. Nowadays the merest tyro in the management of a bicycle, providing he has wit enough to carry with him on his journeys the necessary appli- ances, can generally make good a sudden defect of an ordinary description in the course of a quarter of an hour, and continue his career rejoicing. With regard to lady cyclists,the writer adds that those who are well acquainted witli cycling in all its depart- ments tell me that they anticipate during the coming season, a large accession to the number of lady riders. Last season saw many more on the roads than were ever known before, and, es the proverb says, nothing suc- ceeds like success. It has been proved beyond doubt that cycling does not overtax the strength of the weaker sex, that they find in it a delighful means of recreation, and that it introduces them to many fair scenes that thee would otherwise never visit. Who can be surprised, the-n, that the lady wheelers are a great and a growing body? Some people object to ladies mounting cycles, on the ground that the costumes in which they array themselves are unsuitable. To some extent the objection holds good A flowing skirt is not the most convenient cycling tmj. form,though owing to the manner in which ladies' cycles are constructed it is not as much in the way as may be supposed. Some adventurous dainsels, throwing aside con.ventionality, have boldly adopted knick-rbockers for heir expeditions on wheels. In France more especially his daring flight has been taken. That there is any- lung indecorous in the costume few people will be pre- a-red to atlirm. The change may offend old-fashioned Hoes 1, hit, hut old-fathion.r&notiong shroild by this ueuuus .0 81106t3.

GENERAL NEWS.

Reports received show that great destruction of pro- perty and much loss of life has been cause4hchit Ay in the South and Midlands, by Sonday's gale. A railway wagon, blown from a siding at Bispham station during the gale on Sunday evening,collided with a passenger train from Blackpool, but fortunately little damage was done. Nobody was injured.

HOSPITAL SUNDAY AT WARRINGTON. On Sunday, last week the annual collections were made in the various churches and chapels in Warrington, ;in aid of the Warrington Infirmary. References were made in many of the places of worship, to that useful institution, and appeals were made for funds to enable It to carry on its beneficent work. At Wycliffe Church, the Rev. J. Yonge was the preacher. and in the course ef his remarks, he said that in this town we had a commodious and well-appointed Infirmary,where there was every provision which money and skill could devis e and procure for the treatment of human malady and the mitigation ef suffering. No institution, is seemed to him, was so manifestly philanthropic and Christian as the hospital ; and in few things was the growth of prac- tical Christianity in the present day more fully displayed than in the efforts which were ever being put forth to make as perfect and as replete with comfort as possible institutions in all pArti of the country where physical woes and ills acre ue4lt with and relieved. They might all be sure, therefore, that Christ was as truly honoured and pleased when flis disciples formed and supported hospitals as when they built and supported churches ; and He would have them contribute in His house For the help of physical, as truly as for the help of spiritual, well-being.

STOCKTON HEATII CHURCH LECTURES.

Sir,—The important lecture read at Stockton Heath Institute would be alike interesting and profitable. The subject of Puritan resistance to Church and King, ought to be considered by all Church people and dissenters. Alderman Francomb gave good advice when he urged his hearers to acquaint themselves with " Church His- tory." While there is much to be admired in it there is Much to bo regretted. He said it was remarkable how ignorant some of tbe church people were about church matters. I don't wonder at that, seeing they have n 4 part nor lot in the matter, have no voice in the choice of the clergy nor in the management of church affairs. How difficult it is to keep out of the church the Romanis. lug elements and to keep the erring clergy up to the Protestant faith ! When the Church becomes disestab- lished and disendowed these things will be altered. Alderman Francomb declares the clergy are not State paid and as a proof of this would have us to believe it, because the State balance sheet does not mention it. But this is nip proof and is really absurd. The posses- eions in the hands of the church and managed by it were mostly sanctioned and passed by Act of Parliament. The church was disestablished and disendowed at the Reformation, and re-endowed and re-established by Act of Parliament. Do the church defenders want to know the Acts and decrees which established the church ? The Rector of Louth has been asking Mr. Perks, M.P., to point to an. Act of Parliament that gave the dory of the Church of England their revenues and endowments. The answer vases follows: The Acts are the submission of the clergy Act passed in 1534 compelling the clergy to hold their beneficies; on their submission to the State, and their ful- filment of the canons, and usages of the church as re- formed. The Act of 1535 vesting the supreme headship of the church in the King of England and the Act of 1539 dealing with bishoprics and cathedral bodies and the Act of 1571 legally defining the thirty-nine articles. The Vicar ef Heston says the answer is very simple. The Church of Rome in this country was disendowed, and her revenues handed over to the new Church of England by virtue of stet. 1., Eliz. c. 1., and Eliz. c. 2. 1559, and all who remained of the pre-Reformation Church were deprived of the beneficies, cli4nities and emoluments which were handed over to the post-Reformation Church which declared her predecessors to have been sunk in damnable idolatory. Therefore all these Acts of Parlia- ment are surely a proof if any was wanting, that the clergy are State paid.—Yours truly, A DISSENTER.

THE TREATMENT OF HYDROPHOBIA.

Sir,—There are two most important facts concerning the treatment of hydrophobia which need constantly to be kept before the public. They are, first, that the Pasteur treatment is useless, indeed, worse than useless, in that it often kills of itself; whereas the vapour bath plan of Dr. Buisson not only prevents, but has actually cured the patient when the malady had fastened upon him (Pasteur openly asserts that he cannot cure hydro- phobia), while at the sam,- time it is absolutely harmless. The late Sir Thomas Watson, F.R.C.S., has spoken very highly of the sweating plan, and he said he would have adopted it himself had occasion arisen; Dr. J. H. Clarke assures all people that the evidence (with which no man is better acquainted) is in favour of the vapour baths and against Pasteur's inoculations; and many other medical practitioners agree with Dr. Clarke both as to the Buisson cure and the Pasteur treatment.—Faithfully yours,

" STOCKION HEATH CHURCH HISTORY." Sir,—The sunstance of Mr. Westbrook' s recital of Mr. Lane's lectures on litirch history resklves itself into the following proposition :-.-There was not any change of religion at the Reformation, the Monasteries were robbed, and the inmates evicted, but there was no change in religion, no new church established. Let us test this proposition, by subjecting it to the " search-light " of true, impartial, and disinterested historical facts. Two Bills were prepared at the begin- ning of Elizabeth's reign to present to the new Parlia- ment, the Supremacy Act, restoring to the grown the Royal Supremacy of Henry VIII. (with. certain modifi- cations) over the church ; and the Uniformity Act, en- joining the acceptance of the new religion. The penal- ties for refusing the oath of supremacy were to be fines, imprisonment, and death, as for high treason, and in the case of the clergy deprivation. How invidious the proposed change proved is shown by the " Device for alteration of religion," 3rawn up by the Queen's Secret Council,which says, " Bishops and all the clergy will see their own ruin. In csmfessinn and preaching, and all other ways they can, they will persuade the people from it. (DoricrsChurch History, vol. ii.. Appendix). The House of Lords was packed, two of the bishops were imprisoned, and five others not allowed to take their seats. When the Acts of Supremacy and Uniformity suppressing the practice of the old religion were intro. duced, even in this packed assembly of Peers, it met with long and violent opposition. In the Commons the Supremacy Bill went back to a committee ; " a week

.•

later it was re-introduced, slightly though not materi- ally altered ; and again so strong an opposition followed that it would have been lost except for Cecil, who, in the words of Dr. Fetid, ' flung the question into a gar- boyle,' and carried his point in the confusion." Fronde: History of England, vol. vi., p. 163. The whole bench of English Bishops voted against these Bills, denounced them and opposed them in every way. A strong petition declaring their adherence to the Roman Catholic faith was sent up to Parliament from the Lower House of Convocation ef both provinces and from both , Universities. The first step of opposition in the House of Lords was the presentation of the unanimous petition of the entire spirituality of England, embodying their convictions, the Bishops and Clergy were called upon to renounce the whole Roman Catholic faith, which Abbot Feckenham of Westminster reminded the House of Lords had been" the religion of this island for no less than fourteen hundred years" (Collier ii. 424), and to accept in its place an amalgam of Lutheranism, Calvinism,and Zwinglism. The old service of the Mass, which came into the country with Christianity, was to be abolished under heavy penalties. In its place a communion ser- vice, popularly known as" May Game " (" Green's short history of the English people ") was substituted. The old altars were to be pulled down to emphasize the ex- tinction of the old priesthood, to be replaced by a Calvinistic supper table in the nave of the church, irre- verentantly known as an "oyster-table," and the conse- crated altar-stone marked with its crosses, upon which the Bread of Life had been consecrated for distribution to generations of Englishmen, was to be let into the floor in order that it might be trampled down. All the Bishops of the English Church save one (Kitchen of Llandaff), refused to conform to the new order of things ! The whole of the English Bishops were deposed by the Civil Authority, and is an event unpre- cedented in English history,and settles for ever the ques- tion cf continuity Thus the Ejclesia docens of the Eng- lish Church, being silenced, were supplemented by an unlawful hierarchy of Calvinists of a very pronounced type, who ridiculed Episcopacy and the Apostolic suc- cession. The clergy as a body stuck to their bishops and refused to conform. Nine-tenths of them refused even to appear before the visitors sent to receive their sub- mission. Only 806 of 9,400 Were returned as being will- ing to take the prescribed oath of supremacy. (Lands- downe M.S. c. ix. p. 7.) Thus we see the change of religion effected, by the packing and intimidation of the Lords and the Com- mons, the deposition of the hierarchy, the deprivation of the clergy, the robbery of the monasteries, the des- truction of churches, the desecration of altars, by fires, burnings, and cruelties of the most abonimable kind. We find furthermore the old religion swept away, the name of the Pope erased from the liturgies, the doctrinal and organic conticuity from the early English Church to the time of the rise of anarchy, called or rather miscalled a " Reformation," broken, and we find in its place, as Dr. Short the Protestant historian shows,that, "not only the doctrines but the very words of the confession of Augsburg are incorporated into the thirty-nine Articles. And yet we are coolly told by these " Church Defenders " " that there was no new church," "no change of religion." They may be right in their contention, as Father Vaughan remarks, in the same manner, as a wolf which has devoured a , sheep, can be considered a part of the sheep he has destroyed, or be- cause he wears its fleece I—Yours, &c.

Then the people who have saved have to have recourse to their savings, that is, to buy up all the com- modities they possibly can so that to that extent they relieve the glut that they made by saving. Then a per- tioa of them may obtain employment to produce more. _ _ _

We can see the result of saving on all hands. The workers t2-day make a gift holusbolus of three-fourths of their labour to the small class that are good enough to employ them, and this small class as a result in a very short time is literally crowded out with wealth, which, being altogether disproportionate to its powers of con- sumption, is left on the markets until it has been dis- posed of in some way with the consequences aforesaid. For further effects of this preposterous and exploded fallacy get "A Critical Analysis of Capitalist Produc- tion" by Karl Marx. As to that part of my speech referred to by H. W. in the last paragraph but three of his letter, I have Isere to complain of misrepresentatima. What I said was that in countries where the capitalistic system of producing wealth is most fully developed, then we find the deepest and most widespread poverty and all its concomitants. This statement is borne out not only under our very eyes but by every great writer on the subject. Mr. Henry Heorge says (•• Progress and poverty " p. " Where the population is densest, wealth greatest, and the machinery of production and exchange most fully de- veloped, we find the deepest poverty, the sharpest struggle for existence, and the most enforced idleness." This statement will scarcely admit of argument. It is borne out by almost every economist from Adam Smith downwards. _

Last question : What is Capital ? Capital is the pro- ceeds of past labour devoted to assist present produc- tion for profit. RATEPAYERS' MEETING AT EA RLESTO

The annual meeting of ratepayers of the township of I Newton-in-Makerfield was held in the Town Hall, Earles- town, on Thursday evening. Mr. J. Sankey, overseer, presided, and amongst others present were Councillors Evans, Gilmour, Simpson, Messrs. B. Harrison, J. Clarke,. H. Houghton (assistant overseer), Ste. The notice convening the meeting having been read, the minutes of the last meeting were duly confirmed on the motion of Mr. John Clarke, seconded br Mr. John Robinson.

A VOTE OF CONDOLENCE. The Chairman before calling the assistant overseer to read the accounts, referred in suitable terms tothe death of Mr. G. Ratcliff, who had been his colleague for about nine years.—Mr. B. Harrison seconded, and the motion was unanimously carried.

ACCOUNTS.. The accounts for the first half of the year,ending Oct 13, 1894, showed receipts, including a balanee of £683 2s. 2.1., to be £3,320 17s. 5d., and disbursements £2,740 63. 11d. Ending March 25, 1895, balance £5BO 10s. 6d., total- receipts £2,964 15s. Thd., disbursements £2,456 03 . 3i. , leaving a balance of £5OB 15.3. 4d. to be carried forward.—Mr. Barrett asked why the minute as to the printing of accounts had not been acted upon.—The Assistant Overseer said it was not a jegal thing to do, and further the overseers could not get the accounts printed and sell them at a penny each. The books were all open at any time for any ratepayer to examine them. The accounts were suoject to a Government audit, and any item that the auditor considers is not proper, he would strike it out and surcharge the overseers with it.—Mr. G. Clarke asked if the funds were not to be used for the payment of any deficiency, were the ratepayers prepared to pay the net cost of having a cer-ain number printed ? He took if, the object was not merely to get a balance sheet in the form Mr.Houghton had presented it,but in detail. —The matter then dropped.—Mr.Harrison (auditor) said he and his cAleague had gone over the accounts, and found everything correct,and the books well kept.—The accounts were then passed on the motion of Mr. T. Clare, seconded by Mr. J. Sharpe. APPOINTMENT OF OVERSEERS, kc. The Chairman said it had been customary after the passing of the accounts to appoint certain officers, such as overseers' but an Act had been passed giving the power into the hands of the District Council. He then read a communication from the Local Government Board asking if the overseers had any objection to the power being vested in the Council. He said the overseers did not make any objection, as it seemed to be a general thing that the District Councils were taking the appointment of overseers into their hands, and that was the reason why he did not send a protest against it.—Councillor Gilmour said the appointment of overseers was one of the subjects for discussion at the next meeting of the District Council. That being the first year, he thought it would be courteous on the part of the Council to take the recom- mendations of that meeting, -and he would impress upon ' them the desirability of recommending one gentleman from Earlestown, and one from Newton.—Mr. G. Clarke agreed with the suggestion of Mr. Gilmour.—Councillor Simpson was of a similar opinion, and Mr. Latham then proposed Mr. J. Sankey and Mr. B. Harrison as over- seers for the ensuing year. Mr. W. Clare seconded,and the motion was carried. Mr. H. Houghton was re-appointed assistant over- seer. .

Messrs. D. Williams and D. Forrester were appointed common-loomers ; and Messrs. E. Fairclough and S. Bar- rett were appointed auditors. Messrs. Z. Haidley, J. Bridge, and W. Longton were appointed bu rley men . A voluntary incidental rate of id. in the was laid. This was all the business.

W. GEE.

SOCIALISTS AND LAND NATIONALISATION Sir,—l wonder how it is that such men as your corres pondent "Tony bumpkin " persistently keep on mis- representing the aims and ideas of the Socialists on the laud question. I have read articles and listened to lectures by such men as " Nunquarn,"Leonard Hall,John Trevor and others on the subject, and I never heard or read anything about dividing the land among individ- uals or anything that could be construed to that meaning. We contend as Socialists that the land and all that it contains is God's gift to man and should be worked by the State for the benefit of all the people in it; and not by a few men for their own private ends and profit. Tony rays land nationalisation has not a leg to stand on; but I think it has two good stiardy legs under it. Both before and after the Norman Conquest we find that the land belonged to the King and he granted it to his fol- lowers in return for services to be rendered in time of war. After the tire when the Magna Charta was obtained (which greatly curtailed the power el the King) we find the Barons passing a law and agreeing to pay a tax of 6s. in the pound on the land instead of military service, and afterwards dropping it to 4s. in the pound which has since been allowed to fall into abeyance; and the expense of maintaining the army and navy (which it was raised for) was saddled mainly on the working classes. Our friend tells us' Individualism has been tried and the British Empire is the result," but there are other results which tie does not mention. During the late severe weather we have had the spectacle of thousands of honest men and women and ianocent children starv- ing in the midst of plenty, and frozen to death in the shadow of warehouses filled with warm clothing; condi- tions which Individualists of • Tony bumpkin's' stamp tell us cannot be altered, but which we as Socialists say can and will be altered in the good time coming. HECTOR.

LAND NATIONALISATION.

Sir,—The great "I am"in Tony Lumpkin's,has spoken again, and it is Very evident that the thorn ef the bud bas pricked him in a vital part, for he exclaims" I am a Socialist," and then rambles off in his usual defamatory style of slandering the labour leaders, and using the canting phrases of s acialist mobs, &c., then says these good folks would much rather deal with irrelevancies than calmly examine the subjett in hand. I wonder who it is that is trying to wriggle from the question at issue, for T. L. utterly ignores the propositions and facts I placed before him in my last letter. I ask again is private ownership in land equitable, just, and moral ? Our friend admits the fact that land has been robbed from the nation, and tries to slip out by saying, do two wrongs make one right? T. L. knows well enough ' but he has not the manliness to admittbe fact—he knows that robbery is wrong, and that to despoil the robber of that which he has stolen and give it back to the real owner is richt. Then he goes on to say that our " budding " friend has fallen into the common error of judging one age by the standard of morality of another age. I must admit my ignorance sir, for I only know of one standard of morality, that is equity, justice, and purity, aud any morality outside of this is a sham and a fraud.

And still our great Tony rushes on, claiming that cer- tain names have not a true British ring about them, and possibly the Rosebud may be the product of another soil. Supposing I was, should Ibe any the less a man, should I be any the less a member of this great constit- tion, and should I be any iMe less a good citizen. Shame upon you Tony Lumpkin for your snivelling and con- temptible insinuations. But, Mr. Editor, as you know, I am an Englishman,, and it is because lam one that I love my country and my country's welfare, that I want to see a better state of things in our midst, and it is be- cause I am a worker, and not a sham, that I desire to see my fellowmen in better homes, with better facilities for education and 'developments in all his faculties. It is because I am an Englishman that I wish to bend of the white slaves in our midst, and it is because I am a woik ?r, that I view with alarm, the great commercial stagnation which surrounds us. The thousands of men that are idle, with nothing for them to do, is a question that is with us and will have to be faced, and I think that this question of the land is the one thing which will solve the problem, here again we come to the land.

Just for once to use Tony Lumpkin's style of argument suppose a footpad knocked down travellers and stole his watch, and gave the watch to his sweetheart,who sold it to a Jew, who sold it again to a sailor, and suppose the traveller came forward and claimed the watch, would the law allow this sailor to keep it ? No,but if the foot- pad had been made a peer fur stealing, that would have made all the difference. You may say, of course, that the law of the land has confirtned the old nobility in the possession of their stolen property. Quite true, but it is equally true that the law was made by the landlords themselves, and I claim that if the majority of the people were pri pared through the House of Commons to alter those laws, they would be justified in doing so ? In the eighteenth century the big landlords robbed the small holders in a shameful manner, and within a space of eighty years no less than 7,000,000 acres were enclosed -by men who had no legitimate claim, and when we sug- gest that the land of Eacdand should be restored to the English people, these land robbers have the impudence to raise the cry of plunder. Now let us see,what is land without labour? No value can be got out o it, but through labour. If you doubt this, take a six-acre field, fence it in and wait until the crops grow,vou koow it will never grow unless someone ploughs it and sows it. Also notice what these lords do when they get the land. The late Mr. Stephen Mason, then representing one of the divisions of Lanark- shire, where trade depression was peculiarly severe, instanced the case of a lord highwayman who preys on the minery industry of the district, to the extent of £140,000 per annum. His method of black- mail is this ; lie benignly grants leases for twenty-one rears at fixed rents, varying from £5OO to £5,000. These are paid whether the mine is worked or not. If

worked the royalties come into play. These vary from Id. to Ig. 91. per ton. A still more startling example of landlord jobbery, is to be found in the experience, of the Barrow Hematite Steel Company. It had a share capital of £2,000,000. For years not a penny was made in dividends' but three noble landlords, Devonshire, Buc- cleuch. and Muneastor, divide between them both the site of the town, and the minerals under and around. They received from the company as their dues £126,000 per annum, while the hands who sweltered at the furnaces, had to content themselves, with £63,000 per annum. And in addition to this the hands had to pay , rates, while the three lords got Minot free. Then you talk of foreign competition, aud threaten to lower the wages of the working men. I say men be up and doing; think and judge for yourselves; do not be fooled any linger. You have the power in your own hand. Realm- h.-r that providence helps those who help themselves. Hoping that brighter days may shine on the workers' head is the wish of

Why do I Curse thrift P First, because it, as it is preached to-day, is a fraud and a farce ; and second, be- cause it is incapable of being universally adopted. The industrial class, the workers for whose benefit this modern idea of thrift has beeu invented,is the class that is responsible for the production of all wealth. The pro- duct of the nation (English)in 1882 was £1,500,000,000. Its produce was worth that amount measured in money. (See estimation not of Socialists but R. GiffPri, Pro Marshall, Leoni Levi, Pro Mulhall, Am.) The workers according to the same authorities, take as their share, which againoccording to these statisticians is relatively an ever-diminishing quantity, about a quarter. Now, it does not require extraordinary powers of comprehension to see the inevitable result Of making a man produce £lO worth of wealth and then only giving him purchas- ing power to buy equal to £2 10s. Then the workers are told to save in good times, if a worker can he said to have any—and by good times is meant when the work- ers are literally over-burdened with work—a portion of their earnings. Now,this means that they must abs,ain from the purchase of a certain quantity of commodities, because they do not draw their wages until these corn- „ modities are produced. THE DEATH 01' MR. JOSEPH DAVIES,J.P., OF WARRINGTON. AN INTERESTING CAREER.

Most of our readers will, ere this, be fully con- versant with the lamentable fact that Mr. Joseph Davies J.P., of the Old Warps, Latchford, died late on Friday night last week. Mr. Davies, or to give him the title by which he was best known during his public life, Alderman Davies— has been a confirmed invalid for two or three years,very seldom being seen outside the precincts of his residence, and absolutely precluded from taking any part in the affairs of the town,which:he actively did in former years. In his time the deceased goatleman has been an exceed- ingly hard worker, both in public gratis and in connec- tion with private business, and it is to this excessive labour that his break-down at little over 60 year.i is due Few men were better known, in the town and neighbour- hood, and none in possession of a larger portion of the respect and esteem of the large number of pa sple by whom he was known. Mr. Davies was the son of Mr. and Mrs. John Davies, of Birmingham, and was born on the 24th November, ' 1826, and he was thus in his 69th year atthe time of his death. He was married on the 2nd November, 1848, to Miss Cragg, daughter of the :ate Mr. John Cragg, of Warrington, by whom he had issue five sons, namely, Mr. John Edmund Davies, who is the actuary of the Warrington Savings' Bank; Mr. Percy Davies, solicitor. Sankey-st. ; Mr. Edwin Davies, who died at the Savings' Bank, where Mr. Joseph Davies wag then living, and where he was at the time actuary; Mr. Dennis Davies, who was drowned in the river Mersey, near the Old Warps, in 1867; and Mr. Harry Davies, manager of the Carrs' Colliery, Prescot,of which the deceased gentlemen was one of the owners. The subject of our sketch was one of the trustees of the Warrington Blue Coat School, Winwick-road, and it was at that place where his education was chiefly obtaiaed. Naturally, he always after entertained a strong affection for the institution to which he owed so much,and this was shown in the faithful manner in which he has since guarded its interests. The Blue Coat Bro- therly Smiety owes its foundation to his energy, and he for many years was its president, With the late Mr. Samuel Rigby be was a local director of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Bank, and as an accountant he conducted for many ypars an important business, which, on his retirement; was sold by him to Messrs. T. and J. L. Tunstall and Co., who still carry it on in the same place, Bewsey Chambers. Mr. Joseplsspavies's political life has been correspond- ingly active 6.4 d though by profession he was a Conser- vative he was by no means bigoted in his political 'faith, and never isitated to take an independent course when, in his opinion, the action favoured by his own fotrty was not for the public good. He was elected Mayor of Warrington in 1871-2-3, and during the time he occupied the position of chief magis- trate, he arranged with others—although ;lie took the I most active part in the matter—the purchase of the re- sidence of the late Lord Winmarleigh, which was subse- quently converted into a Town Ball. During the mayor- alty of the late Alderman Webster, the question of the purchase of the gas works arose, and in this Alderman Davies took a most active part as an advocate of the purchase, speaking constantly in the Town Council and at the immerous public meetings in favour of the transfer to the town. Along with other portraits to be seen on the walls of the Mayor's parlour is one of Alderman Davies, depicting him wearing his robe and chain of office ; the likeness is one of great truthfulness. The picture is in oils and was presented to Mr. Davies on his retirement from the position of Mayor. As a churchman, he has filled the office of churchwarden of the Parish Church, and his hon. services, cheerfully rendered for quite 30 years as hon. secretary to the Infirmary and Dispensary, will not readily be forgotten. When the Manchester Ship Canal Scheme was on the tapis Alderman Davies was one of the Provisional Com- mittee. He attended innumerable meetings in London, Manchester, and Warrington, and the scheme ha) no abler exponent. It is a current belief that the undertaking —which he has fortunately lived to see completed—was commenced owing to his having had given to him by the late Lord Winmarleigh a report, written by the late Sir John Rennie, the celebrated engineer, who TO years ago dealt with the question of the advisal ility of making a Ship Canal to Manchester. Being an old member of the Brazennose Club, Manchester, he put this report before his friends; amongst. them being the late Mr. Daniel Adamson. Mr. Davies retired from the Town Council a few years ago, audit will be remembered that only a short time ago, he, together with Mr. John Cros- field, was presented with the freedom of the Borough in recognition of the many services he had rendered to the town. Speaking of Mr. Crosfield, it will not vet have been forgotten, the furore created principally in the Conservative faction, when the deceased gentleman pro- posed Mr. Crostield as Alderman in the year 1881. This act—that of an open-minded man—was considered by the Tories as little better than traitorous conduct, and so incensed were some of the members that one of them, the late Mr. Marsh, then 'councillor, tore the portrait of Mr. Davies from the walls of the Conservative Club and put his foot through it. In public Mr. Davies was a pertinent speaker, and in private a jolly companion. He was a good amateur ac- tor, and was for many years one of the mainstays • the old Warrington Amateur Dramatic Society and ef the Old 1 rafford Club in Manchester. He was the author of several books, one being called "Relaxations of a linsV Life," which is not only well written,but very amusing. His best known vsork,however, is the anmedy,which was played at the Warrington Theatre Royal about the year 1860, and entitled "Our Town, or, the First of Novem- ber." This " trifle," as the author called it, was dedicated to "Colonel Wilsen-Patten, M.P., aide- de-camp to the Queen, Ito., tec., by his most obedient and faithful servant, the author." In regard to local charities, Mr. Davies was a generous and cheerful giver, his name always appearing well up in any public subscription lists,and it is well-known that he gave away considerable sums of money privately in response to the many calls made upon him for (support to churchee, chapels, schools, or any good cause in res- pect of which his aid might be asked. He was the hon. secretary of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary for about 30 years ; and a good idea will be gathered from the above as to the creditable and interesting life lie has led, for the most part in this town.

lIISTORICUS.

THE I.L.P. Sir,—l must appeal to you to exercise that forbear- ance regarding space that characterised the publication of an epistle on the above subject by Mr. H. Whitham in order to afford the same means to justify the existence of a Socialist party distinct from any other party that you afforded H. W. in his denunciation of it, I am not much surprised at the confusion manifested by El. W. when I review the distorted caricature presented of my speech to which he refers, and I promise to be as brief as possible in my reply to him. The necessity for the existence of a Socialist party with a policy distinct from and antagonistic to that of the present political parties is centred in the fact that the industrial problem is not a political but an economic problem, that the great struggle of the future is not between churches and heri- tics, not between republicans and kings, not between Liberals and Tories,but between the propertied and the propertyless, between the privileged and plundered, be- tween the have-ails and do-nothings, and the do-ails and have-nothings. The conscious Socialist is styled by El. W. a victim to a brainless imagination because this motly horde of wiseacres dubbed legislators of the middle-class debating society at Westminster, this curious conclave of religious freethinkers, aristocratic ignoramuses, briefiess barristers, and cunning busixess-sharks has no charm for him, particularly that portion of them labelled Radicals. He (the Socialist) is disgusted with this new-fangled Progressive- Nonconformist, Pie-crust promising—purity—puffey —Liberal—Radical Party. The average member of the self-styled party of the masses evidently imagines that the worker, upon perceiving that his exploitation is the result of a specially prepared concoction generously manipulated by his party, ought to give way to fiercer exultations of joy. But, seriously, what earthly difference can it make to the worLer which of the political parties predominates. Economically they are the same, and politically their real differences are almost imperceptible. Let us look at facts. It is obvious to us—possibly not so to H. W.— that the legislature to-day no longer consists of two real distinct parties i e., of two bodies of men differentiated from eaco other by fundamentally different principles. Home Rule abandoned and the quite minor question of disestablishment ignored,neither of which, by the way, would benefit the workers a red cent. economically,there exists no reason whatever why the simulacrum of party organization should be maintained, or why the struc- tural arrangements of St. Stephen's should not be altered to resemble those of an ordinary lecture ball where all seats face the chair. And with regard to Home Rule the difference of absolute principle is more appar- ent than real. The Gladstonians themselves positively deny any desire of separation and affirm their inten- tion of maintaining the absolute veto of the im- perial parliament, while even the Unionists avow their ultimtte intention of extending to Ireland the same privileges of local government existing in England, and to be enjoyed in Scotland. Again, our legislature at times affords peculiar instances of mere political juggl- ing and real party differences. Let H. W. betake him- self to the British House of Commons on one of these nights. Observe the curious spectacle of one-half of the House wrangling with the other half as to which should. direct the operations of the legal plunder t)f the people. Let him observe the great change in tbe real demeanour of these men at the claim of one of the few labour mem- bers. The labour member claims that the burden im- posed upon the people whom he represents might be palliated by a partial restoration of those natural re- sources of human life that confiscation has robbed him of, and these men who five minutes before were giving vent to their fiery wrath at each other, immediately be- come reconciled, and the labour man is the object of universal opprobrium, Their withering contempt and Loom, their foul imprecations, the moral standard of which unfortunately for some of them limited by what is called Parliamentary etiquette, the latitude of which, however, is at times so stretched that the scene would disgrace a pothouse quarrel,and if it is pushed to a divi- sion the mad rush of both parties to cram one lobby very soon shows the labour man what he is likely to capture from them. If, for instance, lie claima the people's right, through the State, to own the railways which were produced by their labour,and even went so far as to offer as compensation, money enough to build them anew,their amazement at his audacity would soon convince him of the necessity,or rather of the real diffe- rence between the labour party and the privileged party the respective nominees of which call themselves Libe- rals and Tories.

We Socialists visionary as we are, know that political reform ia useless and ineffective 'as a remedy for the pr blem,aad is very much like using liniment and ointment to heal up the breach in a wooden leg. But, says 11,W. I should join the Liberal party in order to influence it. Why not join the 'I ory party for the same reason ? This seems very like appealing to devout believers in Chris- tianity to swell the ranks of the National Atheist S)ciety in order to influence them. Oh, no, H. W. he differ- ences between the Socialist party and the political par- ties are, unlike theirs,realities and not shams. When I read of these constant appeals to the workers to compro- mise with their enemies, to trust to their manly inde- pendence and s forth, I generally experience a peculiar rumbling sensation somewhere about the vicinity of the stomach that is by no means inviting. Fancy the reli- giously-intoxicated missionary among the savages of West Africa endeavouring to compromise with the can- nibals who, having caught him, are clamouring with hunger for the delicious relish a piece of white man affords them. So much for trying to influence the politi- cal proflt-mongers. _

EARLESTOWN LIND NEWTON.

FARLESTOWN MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL. The draw for the honourable position of May Queen in the coming annual feotival, took place at the Mission Hall on Wednesday night. There was a very large number of candidates, the lucky num- ber falling ultimately upon a girl named Isherwood living in Leigh-st., Earlestown. 1.0.G.T. (EBENEZER LODGE). he usual weekly session of the Earlestown Ebenezer Lodge was held in the Co-operative Hall on Tuesday evening. 13r0. J. Taylor, U.T., presided, aud there was a fair attendance of members and friends. The programme of the evening was : Sing, recite, or pay id," Sister Hatton being re- sponsible. The following took part :—Sisters Coombs, Mrs. Witty, Mrs. Taylor, and Hatton ; Bros. Dean, Morris, J. Taylor, Ureenall, and Hop- kins, from Hope of Earlestown Lodge. A very in- teresting programme was conclu4eel at 9.30. Guth) TEMPLARY.

The weekly session of the Hope of Earlestown lodge of Good Templars was held lit the Co-opera- tive Hall, on Monday evening. Bro. J. Miller presided, and there was a very large attendance. Five candidates were admitted to membership, and several others proposed. The digest of business for No. 1 Convention to be held at Earlestown on Sat- urday votiek was read by the Secretary. The pro- gramme of the evening was "Auction night." A varied assortment of articles whicn had been given by members were sold for the benefit of the lodge, Bro. W..fluddleston officiating as auctioneer.

VULCAN FOUNDRY INFANT SCHOOL.

The Vloc—e-s—a-n Inspector's report on the religious examination held recently at the School, which is under the charge of Miss M. E. Lath waite has come to hand, and is as follows :—" Presented in two groups :—The work in this school is evidently very thorough and sympathetic. The children have been well taught, and the answering was very general. The amount included in the work offered, was also extensive for the little ones in both groups. The old and new testament have been taught well; the child- ren often gave me the exact words of the Bible as a natural answer to my question. The texts given for repetition were well known, and the hymns very nicely and sweetly sung; the collects and prayers were reverently said. A few children also took a little written work, which was well done. The work is evidently carried on in a thoroughly satisfactory manner. Present 105, average 83. NEwTON DIVISION BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

The annual general meeting of the above associa- tion was held at the Pied Bull Hotel on Saturday evening. Owing, no doubt to the inclement state of the weather, only six clubs were represented viz., Newton, Earlestown, Newton Nursery, Gol- borne, Haydock and Notth Ashton. Mr. T. leloly- neux, of Golborne, presided. The minutes of the last meeting were read, and confirmed, anl Mr. John Jackson, treasurer, presented a balance sheet which showed that the season opened with an adverse balance of ..£1 7s. 4d. ; the total income amounted to £2l 13s. hid. The balance sheet was adopted. Mr. T. W. Legh, M.P., was elected presi- dent, Mr. John Robinson, vice-president, Mr. John Jackson, treasurer. Mr. Crompton tendered his resignation as secretary, saying that he could not devote the time he wished, during the summer months, and it was with regret that be had to resign. AEU r some discussion the resignation was accepted, and Mr. E. Grace was elected secretary. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded from the chair, to Mr. Crompton for his past services. The draw for the games during the season then took place,each.club having to play on neutral greens.
